A Deep Dive Into Hailey Bieber’s Many, Many Face Masks

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

“Dealing with my issues one mask a time,” wrote Hailey Bieber beneath a recent Instagram carousel. While she’s not elaborating on said issues, her extensive collection of face masks have been exposed for every skincare obsessive’s over-analysis. Hailey Bieber has a face mask for every occasion and skin concern, it seems.

There she is, covered in mustard-yellow paste, slathered in a thick white cream, encased in a glossy lavender-grey treatment, and illuminated by the electric-blue glow of an LED device. There are hydrogel sheets, eye patches, and a rather arresting cobalt-blue mask from a recent trip to Seoul. Bieber is clearly not afraid to cast the net wide in pursuit of good skin.

Of course, we’ve long known Bieber as the reigning queen of glazed-donut skin. But beyond the perfectly dewy complexion and the seemingly endless supply of Rhode products, she’s a genuine skincare fanatic. And, someone who has been particularly candid about the challenges of maintaining her skin. Bieber has previously described her skin as dry, sensitive, and easily irritated, particularly by fragrances, harsh formulas, and certain sunscreens. She’s also spoken about her ongoing struggles with perioral dermatitis, an inflammatory condition that can cause uncomfortable redness, bumps, and flaking around the mouth and nose. Frequent travel, stress, hormonal fluctuations, and unfamiliar products can all contribute to her flare-ups.

As she’s previously explained, her approach when her skin is irritated is minimal: rinsing with water, using prescribed azelaic acid and clindamycin, hypochlorous acid, and Rhode’s ceramide-rich Glazing Milk. In her 2025 Beauty Secrets video, she also revealed that she generally skips a full morning cleanse in favor of a water rinse, saving the heavier-duty moisturizing for nighttime.

All of which explains a lot about her mask collection. Hydration, calming, barrier support, and a little depuffing appear to be the common threads. Although, importantly, a mask that hydrates dry skin won’t necessarily treat perioral dermatitis—and some formulas can aggravate it. So, what exactly is she using?

The first one I can immediately pinpoint is the bright-blue mask Bieber wore during her recent visit to South Korea: PYDERIN’s Hydrovuster PDRN SOS Hyal Soothing Mask. Sold largely through Korean pharmacies and dermatology clinics, it belongs to a new wave of PDRN-focused skincare products. PDRN, or polydeoxyribonucleotide, is derived from DNA fragments, commonly sourced from salmon, and has become a particularly buzzy ingredient in Korean regenerative skincare. The mask uses 10 forms of hyaluronic acid to attract and retain moisture, alongside ingredients such as panthenol, azulene, and barrier-supporting compounds. The distinctive blue tint comes from an azulene derivative, associated with soothing properties. The idea is to leave dehydrated, stressed skin feeling calmer, plumper, and more comfortable. It’s worth distinguishing topical PDRN from the injectable treatments offered in clinics, though: a sheet mask cannot promise the same regenerative results.

Then there’s Rhode’s Caffeine Reset Sculpting Cream Mask, which Bieber developed over approximately 18 months and more than 50 iterations. The rich, white rinse-off formula is designed as a morning pick-me-up for tired, puffy skin. “A cup of coffee for your face,” is what the Rhode copy says. Caffeine helps temporarily reduce the appearance of puffiness, while peptides, poppy extract, and phytosterols target radiance, surface hydration, and a firmer-looking complexion. Vanillyl butyl ether provides an invigorating sensation.

The brand’s other masking essential that I can spy in the IG carousel is the Peptide Eye Prep: cooling hydrogel under-eye patches. Formulated with caffeine, peptides, glycerin, and sodium PCA, they’re intended to hydrate the delicate under-eye area, temporarily minimize puffiness, and soften the appearance of fine lines. They are also super practical: Bieber has previously told Vogue Business that she wears hers to the gym or when heading out for coffee.

Hailey Bieber’s mask habit predates Rhode by several years. Among her earlier favorites are two treatments from Dr. Barbara Sturm. The original Face Mask combines aloe vera, chamomile, purslane, and kaolin clay for a combination of moisture, softness, and soothing care. Its Clarifying Mask, meanwhile, is more targeted towards blemish-prone skin, using zinc and kaolin to absorb excess sebum, alongside botanical ingredients intended to support the skin barrier. The latter is more of an occasional clarifying treatment than an everyday solution for reactive skin.

She’s also previously expressed her love for Drunk Elephant’s F-Balm Electrolyte Waterfacial, an overnight hydration treatment packed with humectants, squalane, niacinamide, and barrier-supporting lipids. It basically extends your nighttime mositurizing routine, as it helps the skin hold on to water while you sleep.

Her more recent masking repertoire also encapsulates the ongoing collagen obsession. One slide looks like Medicube’s PDRN Pink Collagen Gel Mask, a long-wear hydrogel treatment designed to boost hydration and give skin a visibly plumper finish. The brand’s Collagen Night Wrapping Mask offers a different experience: a liquid treatment that dries into a thin film, worn overnight to help retain moisture before being peeled or rinsed away in the morning. Despite the collagen branding, these are principally about surface hydration, softness, and the temporary appearance of bouncier skin, rather than replacing the collagen naturally found deeper in the skin.

And let’s not forget LOOPS’s Double Take Glow Mask, another Bieber-approved hydrogel treatment. This one brings together sunflower seed oil, aloe vera, and hyaluronic acid for a quick hit of hydration and radiance. With a suggested wear time of just 10 minutes, it’s the kind of mask you could don pre-makeup and a night out.

The assortment also includes treatments that are harder to identify from a selfie alone. The glossy, pale grey-blue mask pictured during what appears to be a professional facial resembles an alginate or rubberizing treatment, which is typically applied as a thick paste and allowed to set before removal. Such treatments can help retain moisture against the skin, although the exact benefits depend on their ingredients. The yellow and lavender-grey formulas remain mysteries for now.

Bieber’s carousel also features an LED light-therapy mask, another fixture of the increasingly high-tech celebrity skincare routine. Red and near-infrared light are commonly used to support collagen production and improve the appearance of fine lines and skin texture over time, while blue light is used for certain forms of acne. The precise device and light setting in Bieber's photograph aren’t clear (although we know her bestie Kendall loves her TheraFace Mask Glo), but the benefits of these treatments generally depend on consistency rather than a single session.

Bieber’s routine, of course, extends beyond what comes in a jar or compressed between plastic. She’s previously discussed PRP and PRF treatments, microneedling, and occasional laser sessions.

For anyone inspired to amp up their own mask collection, the important thing is to choose according to what your skin actually needs. Hyaluronic acid, glycerin, ceramides, and other hydrating ingredients make sense for tight, dehydrated skin; caffeine-based treatments can provide a temporary depuffing effect; and clarifying clay masks can be useful for excess oil, provided they don’t leave the skin feeling stripped. Apply hydrating sheet masks after cleansing and before moisturizer, unless the product directs otherwise. Cream masks generally need rinsing, while overnight wraps are designed to be the final step of an evening routine. Follow the recommended wear time: leaving a mask on longer doesn't automatically make it more effective. Introduce new formulas gradually, especially if your skin is reactive.

And while Bieber makes the case for having a mask for every occasion, those with active perioral dermatitis should be more selective. Heavy, occlusive products and unfamiliar ingredients can worsen the condition, and the best approach during a flare-up may be to forgo extra masking entirely in favor of a simple, dermatologist-guided routine.

Ultimately, the lesson from Hailey Bieber’s collection isn’t that you need eight different masks to achieve good skin. It’s that skincare is rarely one-size-fits-all, even for the woman who helped turn a glossy complexion into a billion-dollar beauty business.

New And Recent Movies Streaming: Big Films We Can Finally Watch At Home - Updated November 2026

Mary-Kate and Ashley Olsen may be best known for their shared role as Michelle Tanner on Full House, but the child actors -- who eventually left Hollywood -- also launched their own cinematic universe of sorts. In the '90s and early 2000s, the duo starred in several TV and direct-to-video movies. For the longest time, most of these movies were only available to fans who owned the hard copies, but that’s starting to change as a handful of their movies have popped up on Prime Video (though one of my favorite titles hasn't been added).

Unlike most movies that are released, the highly successful Olsen twins managed to retain all the rights to a majority of their films after buying out the co-founder and former manager of their entertainment company, making them their own bosses. This allowed Mary-Kate and Ashley to control how their films were distributed and thus kept them off streaming services. It’s unclear what has changed, but it seems like the Olsens have realized how sought-after these films are to their millennial fans and have started to loosen the reins. As a fan of the Mary-Kate and Ashley Cinematic Universe, I’m ecstatic.

As of writing this, eight Mary-Kate and Ashley Olsen movies are currently available to stream for Prime Video subscription holders. Four of them are from the early days of the twins’ career and were filmed while Full House was still airing new seasons. The titles include To Grandmother’s House We Go, a Christmas movie and the first of their television films; the Halloween flick Double Double Toil and Trouble; It Takes Two, a loose book-to-screen adaptation of Mark Twain’s The Prince and the Pauper; and How the West Was Fun.

There are also four films from the twins’ post-Full House days, including Passport to Paris, Billboard Dad, Switching Goals and one of my personal favorites and their final movie, New York Minute. Needless to say, this is a sweet lineup, but can I just take a moment to highlight one flick that's not present?

While the rest of us were being "normal kids," the Olsen twins were busy producing and starring in a total of fourteen movies between 1992 and 2004. Eight films aren't a bad start, but there are still a lot that need to be added to complete the Mary-Kate and Ashley Cinematic Universe. However, there is one movie that I need to be added ASAP: the 2001 flick Holiday in the Sun.

In the film, Mary-Kate and Ashley play the Stewart twins, whose parents surprise them with a trip to Atlantis Resort in the Bahamas over their winter break. I vividly remember being in awe, listening to “Island in the Sun” play as the twins jet off to the Caribbean. And if Prime Video needs another reason to get the rights to Holiday in the Sun ASAP, it also serves as Megan Fox’s film debut.

As bummed as I and several other Olsen twin fans are that Holiday in the Sun isn’t one of the eight featured on Prime Video (as of now), it doesn’t put too much of a damper on the reveal. After all, we’ve waited years for even one of their films to hit streaming services, and now we have access to half their filmography.

Despite there being no official word from Prime Video, news of the release did travel fast on social media, with fans sharing their joy on X:

I guess it’s time for me to pause my marathon of watching all the new and upcoming Christmas movies and head over to Prime Video to revisit some of these gems. Maybe if enough of us watch, the streamer will add Holiday in the Sun or any of the other Olsen twins movies!

I’m Not Rooting For JD And Elliot To Get Back Together On Scrubs (At Least Not This Week)

The Nobody Wants This Cast Proposed A New Lead For Season 4 (I Need This To Happen)

Jamie Lee Curtis’s New TV Show Is Giving Me All The ‘90s Sitcom Nostalgia, But Now I’m Worried

90 Day: Hunt For Love Has Me Thinking One New Couple May Split Before The Season Ends

SPOILERS are ahead for Scrubs Season 2 through episode 8. Catch up with a Hulu subscription before reading ahead!

Ever since the Scrubs revival made its way onto the 2026 TV schedule, I’ve been tuning in, and it really hasn’t disappointed. Last season was good, but I think Season 2 has been especially stellar, and that has a lot to do with what subject matter is getting the spotlight. The new season reeled me in with Dr. Cox’s health developments, but now I’m really hooked thanks to what’s going on with J.D. and Elliot’s relationship. And, I think it’s time I discuss it.

When Scrubs returned to TV, I was instantly taken aback by the choice to make J.D. and Elliot exes. Bill Lawrence defended the decision to CinemaBlend months ago, saying that the couple just couldn’t go a few episodes without “being a horrible couple,” so he wanted to be realistic.

While it bummed me out at first, I’m starting to really appreciate the sort of nuanced conversations that come with J.D. and Elliot no longer being together. They’re both still co-workers and co-parents, so they have to make it work as friends, but I just don’t feel like I see enough relationships like this on TV. My own parents have been divorced for awhile, and I’ve watched their relationship evolve in a new way amidst still being my parents. I know it’s quite common, and yet, for many TV couples it’s either them being together or not.

On that topic, the episode from a couple of weeks ago, “My Story,” really threw me for a loop in the best way. In it, we’re led to believe that J.D. and Elliot end up going to dinner together and deciding to try to make things work again. We learned more about why things fell apart between them, too. But ultimately, we learn the whole scenario is happening in J.D.’s head, and he decides not to pursue their relationship in fear of hurting her once again.

I’m all for love, and a part of me wants to see these two back together, but I'm also really enjoying seeing this unique dynamic on Scrubs. There’s clearly a lot of real love between them, but they were also famously on-again-off again throughout the original run of Scrubs. It feels like we’re getting to watch these two attempt to heal in real time and decide how to go forward in their lives, between being busy medical professionals and being parents, too.

I guess for the time being I’m not thinking about whether I want them together ultimately or not, I just want to continue to see this difficult dynamic between them be explored. I think Scrubs is one of those few shows that can simultaneously be hilarious and emotional, and I think what’s going on between J.D. and Elliot is something worth stretching out for a while.

How Much Does It Cost To Charter A Pilatus PC-12 Per Hour In 2026?

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Niklas Engel | Shutterstock | Simple Flying

Brandon's passion for aviation started at a young age. He became involved in the flight simulator and VATSIM communities, which sparked his interest in aviation and eventually led him to earn his private pilot license. Outside of work, he continues to build flight hours, explore new airports, and stay involved in the aviation community.

Chartering a Pilatus PC-12 costs roughly $2,000 to $3,000 per hour in 2026, according to Simple Flying's analysis of private charter pricing drawing on figures from BlackJet and Amalfi Jets. That places the aircraft in the turboprop category alongside the Beechcraft King Air 350i, carrying six to nine passengers on missions up to around 1,500 miles (2,400 km). It is the cheapest way to charter a crewed aircraft of that size.

The hourly rate is also the least reliable number in private aviation. Charter brokers publish PC-12 figures anywhere between $1,000 and $3,500 per hour, and the gap exists because the rate is a starting point rather than a price. Positioning flights to bring the aircraft to the departure airport are billable, which can triple the cost of a short trip. Taxes, fuel surcharges, landing fees, crew overnight expenses and seasonal demand all sit on top. The aircraft is priced by the flight hour for the entire aircraft rather than per passenger, which means a full nine-seat cabin brings the per-person cost below $280 an hour while a solo traveler pays the whole rate.

Simple Flying's analysis of private jet charter pricing in 2026 places turboprops, including the Pilatus PC-12 and the Beechcraft King Air 350i at $2,000 to $3,000 per hour, drawing on figures from charter operators BlackJet and Amalfi Jets. That is the most defensible number available, and it sits roughly in the middle of what the wider market publishes.

The spread around it is considerable. Charter brokers quoting the PC-12 online put the figure anywhere between $1,000 and $3,500 per hour depending on which page you read, and some publish multiple conflicting ranges across their own site. A prospective customer searching the aircraft will find figures that differ by a factor of three for what is nominally the same product, which says more about how the charter market advertises itself than about what the aircraft costs.

The variation is not purely marketing. An hourly rate for a charter aircraft is a quoted starting point rather than a fixed price, and the figure an operator publishes reflects its own cost base. Aircraft age matters. Base location matters, because an aircraft positioned near the customer flies fewer empty miles to reach them. Cabin configuration, operator overhead, and seasonal demand all feed in. Simple Flying notes that taxes, fuel surcharges, positioning costs, airport fees, and seasonal demand can all dramatically alter the final invoice, which means the hourly rate describes one input into a quote rather than the quote itself.

Private charter is priced by the flight hour for the aircraft rather than per passenger. That structure defines the economics of the category. A PC-12 costs the same whether one person flies or the cabin is full, which means the per-person figure falls as passengers are added and the solo traveler pays the entire rate alone.

Simple Flying makes that point directly, noting that passenger count is what actually determines value rather than the headline hourly figure. Two people on a turboprop at $2,500 an hour are each effectively paying $1,250. The same aircraft carrying eight brings that below $320. Nothing about the aircraft or the flight changes. Only the division does. That is the calculation behind most group charter bookings, where a full cabin can bring the per-person cost within range of a refundable business class fare.

Fuel is a smaller component than most people assume. AOPA recorded the PC-12 NGX burning 414 to 416 pounds per hour at maximum continuous power at 27,000 feet, which works out to roughly 62 gallons an hour. Against an hourly charter rate of $2,500, fuel accounts for a few hundred dollars at most. The balance covers the operator's cost base spread across the hours it expects to fly, including aircraft ownership or financing, maintenance reserves, insurance, crew salaries and training. That is why two operators quoting the same aircraft type arrive at different numbers. The hourly rate is where a business model becomes a price, and the businesses behind identical aircraft are not identical.

The PC-24 is good all-around aircraft and still in demand today.

The advertised hourly rate is one line on an invoice that usually contains several. Simple Flying lists taxes, fuel surcharges, positioning costs, airport fees, and seasonal demand as factors that can dramatically alter the final figure, and any of them can move a quote by thousands of dollars on a short trip.

Positioning is the one that catches people out. Charter aircraft are based somewhere, and if that somewhere is not the customer's departure airport, the aircraft has to fly empty to reach them and frequently empty to return afterward. Those ferry legs are billable. A two-hour trip on an aircraft based two hours away can be charged as six hours of flying, which triples the cost of the mission without the customer spending any longer in the air. It is also why empty-leg pricing exists, since an operator repositioning an aircraft anyway would rather sell those hours cheaply than fly them for nothing, and it is why basing matters more to a charter quote than most customers expect.

The remaining items accumulate. Landing fees, ramp and handling charges, overnight parking, catering beyond basic provisions, and crew expenses if the trip requires an overnight stay all appear separately. Peak periods carry premiums, with holiday weekends, major sporting events and ski season pushing rates above published figures in the markets concerned. A PC-12 quoted at $2,500 per hour on a weekday in March is not the same aircraft quoted at the same rate on a Friday before Thanksgiving, which is why the hourly figure is best treated as the starting point of a conversation rather than the answer to one.

Simple Flying's category breakdown places turboprops, including the PC-12 and King Air 350i, at $2,000 to $3,000 per hour, carrying six to nine passengers on missions up to roughly 1,500 miles (2,400 km). That is the entry point to private charter on a crewed aircraft of meaningful size, and the only category where the hourly figure starts with a two.

Light jets come next, at approximately $2,500 to $4,500 per hour according to figures from BlackJet and Amalfi Jets. The step up buys speed and a jet cabin rather than a substantial increase in passenger capacity, since most light jets seat four to eight. Midsize and super-midsize jets dominate the longer end of the charter market at $4,000 to $8,000 per hour, with many operators quoting around $6,500 for popular super-midsize models. At the top, the largest ultra-long-range business jets exceed $20,000 per hour, which is roughly 10 times what the same trip would cost on a turboprop if the turboprop could fly it.

The capability difference does not scale evenly with that ratio. A turboprop at $2,500 and a super-midsize at $6,500 both carry a similar number of passengers. What the additional $4,000 per hour buys is range, cabin volume and speed, which matter on a four-hour sector and matter very little on a 45-minute one. Simple Flying makes the same point about passenger count, noting that what actually determines value is how many people are aboard rather than the headline rate. A heavy jet at $14,000 per hour split across 14 passengers costs less per person than a turboprop at $2,500 carrying two.

The case for a turboprop over a jet rests on where the aircraft can land rather than how fast it flies. The PC-12 is certified for single-pilot operation and can take off and land on short, unpaved airstrips as short as 760 meters, according to Oriens Aviation. Takeoff distance over a 50-foot obstacle is 2,485 feet (757 meters), with landing distance over the same obstacle at 2,170 feet (661 meters). That lets it serve airports a light jet would have to overfly, which means the relevant comparison for a passenger heading to a mountain town or a remote resort is total door-to-door time including the drive at the far end, not cruise speed.

The performance is closer to a jet than the category suggests. The PC-12 NGX cruises at a maximum of 290 KTAS on a single 1,200-shaft-horsepower Pratt & Whitney PT6E-67XP, climbs at 1,920 feet per minute at sea level, and operates up to 30,000 feet. AOPA noted the NGX gained five knots over earlier PC-12 models through more precise digital torque limiting, which Pilatus says permits up to 10% more thrust in climb and cruise. Maximum range is 1,803 nautical miles with four passengers.

The pricing follows the same logic. At $2,000 to $3,000 per hour against $2,500 to $4,500 for a light jet, the turboprop costs less for similar capacity. The jet earns its premium when the sector is long enough for speed to matter, when the destination has a runway that supports it, or when several hours in a jet cabin is worth paying for. Outside those conditions, the turboprop is frequently the better aircraft rather than the cheaper compromise.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Boeing Is Handing 3 Businesses to Archer Aviation and Closing a $131 Billion F-15 Order. Here’s Which Stock Gets the Better Deal.

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Boeing offers stability, but Archer offers greater upside.

Boeing (BA -0.03%) is selling three aerospace businesses to Archer Aviation (ACHR -6.93%), giving the electric aircraft developer an established defense operation generating more than $200 million in annual revenue. Meanwhile, Boeing has secured a U.S. military contract with a maximum value of $131.23 billion to support its F-15 fighter jet program.

Both developments could significantly improve the companies' long-term prospects. But they represent very different opportunities. Archer is gaining revenue, technology, and a stronger foothold in defense. Boeing is strengthening an already substantial military business while simplifying its operations.

And while both deals look promising, one stock offers a more compelling opportunity. Let’s find out which one.

In August, Boeing agreed to sell Archer three subsidiaries: Wisk Aero, Insitu, and SkyGrid. Each brings something different.

Wisk develops autonomous electric aircraft capable of taking off and landing vertically, similar to helicopters. Its technology could eventually help Archer develop air taxis that operate without pilots. SkyGrid develops software for managing air traffic, including coordinating autonomous aircraft and drones.

But Insitu is arguably the most valuable addition. That company manufactures military drones used for intelligence gathering, surveillance, and reconnaissance. Unlike Archer's emerging air taxi business, Insitu already generates revenue and operates profitably.

According to Boeing, Insitu produces more than $200 million in annual revenue and operates across 35 countries. That's a substantial addition for Archer, which generated just $5.1 million in revenue during Q3 2026. The acquisition could also help Archer expand its defense business beyond the electric air taxis that originally attracted investors.

Archer is already developing autonomous aircraft with defense technology company Anduril. Its Halo and Thunder platforms are intended for commercial and military applications, respectively. Adding Insitu's manufacturing experience, customer relationships, and established drone business could accelerate those efforts.

Boeing isn't receiving cash for the three subsidiaries. Instead, it will receive newly issued Archer shares equal to approximately 19.75% of Archer's pre-transaction Class A share count, plus warrants that could allow it to acquire additional shares. Boeing will also retain access to Wisk's autonomous flight technology through a collaboration agreement.

Essentially, Boeing is exchanging direct ownership of three businesses for a substantial investment in Archer. That gives Boeing exposure to autonomous aviation without having to fund the subsidiaries' development on its own.

For Archer, however, the deal comes with dilution. Still, Archer is getting something it desperately needs: an established, revenue-producing operation.

In August, Boeing received a contract covering production, upgrades, maintenance, and support for the F-15 fighter jet. The agreement carries a maximum value of $131.23 billion and could support work extending into the next decade.

To be sure, Boeing hasn't received a guaranteed $131 billion order. The agreement is an indefinite-delivery, indefinite-quantity contract. It allows the government to place orders over time. Actual revenue will depend on how much work the government orders and funds.

That said, the contract strengthens Boeing's position in military aviation. And make no mistake: Boeing's defense business is already showing improvement.

Q3 defense revenue increased 15% to approximately $7.9 billion, while operating earnings reached $492 million. The company also reported a record $745 billion total backlog across its businesses. Those numbers provide a much stronger financial foundation than Archer currently has.

Boeing reported Q3 revenue of $24.2 billion, up 4% from the previous year. Operating cash flow reached approximately $1.5 billion, while free cash flow totaled $464 million. But Boeing isn't completely out of the woods. Its commercial airplane division still recorded a $697 million operating loss during the quarter. That's not trivial.

The company also carried approximately $45.9 billion in debt against $20.7 billion in cash and marketable securities. Indeed, improving aircraft production and profitability will remain essential to Boeing's recovery.

I'd give Archer the edge if you're willing to accept substantial risk. The Boeing acquisition could transform Archer from a largely pre-commercial air taxi developer into a diversified aerospace and defense company with meaningful existing revenue.

Of course, its Q3 net loss of $274.6 million shows how expensive development remains. Certification delays, continued cash consumption, and shareholder dilution could still weigh heavily on the stock. Boeing offers a different proposition.

Its enormous backlog, improving cash flow, and established military programs make it the more financially mature business. But its size also means the Archer transaction is unlikely to dramatically change its overall earnings trajectory.

Boeing is definitely the more established choice, particularly for those who want exposure to aerospace and defense without depending on the successful commercialization of electric air taxis. Archer has more to prove. But the three Boeing businesses could give it a considerably stronger starting point.

'Out there, moving into the unknown': As Voyager 1 reaches one light-day from Earth, scientists reflect on its past — and its future

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nearly 50 years after launch, NASA's Voyager 1 probe will pass the one light-day mark on Nov. 18, a symbolic milestone for the agency's longest-running mission. Scientists reflect on the Voyager program's legacy and what comes next.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

NASA's Voyager 1 probe, already the farthest human-made object in space, is about to set another record. On Nov. 18, the spacecraft will be so far from Earth that light, the universe's ultimate speed limit, takes a full 24 hours to reach it.

For researchers who worked on the Voyager program and continue to study its data, the symbolic milestone of being a full light-day from Earth celebrates a mission that continues half a century after it began. Since launching in September 1977, the twin Voyager probes have outlasted many of the engineers who built them, and their flybys of the outer solar system planets remain some of the best close-up observations scientists have of those distant worlds.

"Voyager 1 and Voyager 2 are transcending generations," Pascal Lee, a planetary scientist at the SETI Institute who was part of the Voyager 2 team at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) during its Neptune flyby in 1989, told Live Science. "Not only is their data relevant, but they themselves are still out there, moving into the unknown."

Here is what the Voyagers have revealed about the solar system and interstellar space, what happens when their power finally runs out, and whether anyone — or anything — might ever intercept the messages from Earth they carry.

A NASA simulation showing where Voyager 1 is relative to the sun and solar system planets.

Voyager 1, cruising through space at nearly 38,000 mph (61,000 km/h), is just shy of 16 billion miles (about 26 billion kilometers) from Earth. That's the distance light travels in 24 hours, also known as one light-day. The spacecraft will reach this milestone on Nov. 18 at 5:16:07 a.m. ET, after traveling through space for 49 years, two months and 11 days.

As long as that sounds, "it's still pathetically slow in the grand scheme of things," Lee told Live Science.

If anything, it reminds us of the sheer vastness of space and time, and how this spacecraft is just a little mote of dust that's barely drifted out of the solar system still.

Scientifically speaking, Voyager 1 hasn't even left its home neighborhood yet. In 2012, the probe traveled beyond the heliosphere, the bubble of plasma blown outward by the sun that shields the planets from incoming cosmic radiation. But it still remains within the sun's gravitational grasp, which extends past the Oort Cloud — a vast spherical shell of icy bodies surrounding the solar system. Voyager 1 will need about 300 more years just to reach the cloud's inner edge, and possibly 30,000 years to fly beyond it, according to NASA.

"If anything, it reminds us of the sheer vastness of space and time, and how this spacecraft is just a little mote of dust that's barely drifted out of the solar system still," Lee said.

On its current path, Voyager 1 will pass within 1.7 light-years of a dim star called Gliese 445 in about 40,000 years, at which point it will be closer to that star than to our own sun. Farther out, the nearest star system to our own, Proxima Centauri, sits about 4.24 light-years away — more than 1,500 times farther than Voyager 1 will be on Nov. 18.

Meanwhile Voyager 1's twin, Voyager 2, took a different route through the solar system after both probes launched in 1977, in order to swing past Uranus and Neptune after revisiting Jupiter and Saturn. Currently in interstellar space about 13 billion miles (21 million km) from Earth, Voyager 2 will reach its own light-day mark in November 2035, according to NASA.

An illustration of the Voyager probes passing beyond the heliopause, into interstellar space.

Crossing the one-light-day mark doesn't change how NASA communicates with the probe, though it does demand more patience.

NASA talks to the spacecraft using radio signals, which travel at the speed of light. At a distance of one light-day, a signal sent from Earth takes 24 hours to reach Voyager 1, and a reply takes another 24 hours to make the return journey.

"If you want to say 'hello' to Voyager, you send your message and then you go home and you wait two days before you finally get a response," Suzanne Dodd, Voyager project manager at JPL, said in a recent statement.

As the spacecraft continues to cruise deeper into space, that wait will grow longer. Moreover, 50 years of traversing the solar system and the increasing distance from our sun takes a toll on the probe itself. Because sunlight in the outer reaches is far too faint for solar panels, Voyager 1 relies on nuclear batteries that convert heat from the natural decay of plutonium into electricity. That supply is gradually dwindling, prompting NASA to gradually shut down nonessential instruments to conserve what energy remains and extend the mission as much as possible.

Kelly Perry, a data systems engineer on the Voyager program at JPL, told Live Science via email that the mission team's priority remains the same: "keep the spacecraft going as long as possible."

A montage of planets imaged by the twin Voyager probes, showing Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune.

Before the Voyagers launched, the outer solar system was largely a blur of telescopic speculation. The twin probes took advantage of a planetary alignment that comes around only once every 176 years, using the gravity of giant planets to slingshot themselves onward.

Together, they have discovered 23 moons across the outer solar system, revealed faint rings around Jupiter and captured the first detailed atmospheric profiles of Saturn, Uranus and Neptune. They also gave the first detailed, close-up views of Jupiter's moon Europa, transforming our perception of it from a blurry speck into a remarkably smooth, geologically young world — and now one of the prime candidates for finding extraterrestrial life.

We had no idea what they looked like. And all of a sudden they became places we could map, imagine ourselves exploring, landing on…they became places.

Among the more striking finds, Voyager 1 spotted active volcanoes on Jupiter's moon Io in 1979, the first time they had been seen on any world other than Earth.

"We had no idea what they looked like," Lee said. "And all of a sudden they became places we could map, imagine ourselves exploring, landing on…they became places," he added.

"That's what Voyager was for me — a voyage of discovery," he said.

These discoveries laid the groundwork for decades of follow-up exploration, including NASA's Galileo mission to Jupiter and the Cassini mission to Saturn. Decades later, as the only spacecraft to return in situ data from outside the heliosphere, the probes also proved that interstellar space is a dynamic, complex frontier rather than an empty vacuum.

Each Voyager spacecraft launched with 10 science instruments. Today, only two remain active on Voyager 1 and three on Voyager 2, measuring the magnetic fields, plasma and cosmic rays of interstellar space.

NASA began switching off the others one by one decades ago to conserve power, including the camera on Voyager 1 just minutes after it snapped the famous "Pale Blue Dot" portrait of the solar system in 1990. Ongoing power-saving reconfigurations have since helped keep the aging spacecraft operational nearly 50 years into its lifetime.

Voyager's iconic "Pale Blue Dot" image shows Earth as a "mote of dust suspended in a sunbeam," in the words of Carl Sagan.

In one such recent effort, nicknamed the "Big Bang," engineers shut off certain high-power subsystems while switching on lower-power alternatives, which "bought us almost two more years during which we will not need to turn any instrument off," Perry said.

With onboard power steadily dwindling, however, "there's a recognition that it won't be forever," Deana Weibel, an anthropologist at the Grand Valley State University in Michigan who co-authored an upcoming book about the Golden Record aboard the Voyager probes, told Live Science.

Because Voyager is a science mission, its mission will be considered finished when "no science instruments are operational," Perry said. "The Science Team decided that the last remaining instrument will be the magnetometer," she added. "We will never intentionally power off this instrument."

After the science data eventually stops, both probes may still keep beaming back basic engineering updates, such as about their health and power status, until around 2036, according to NASA. Even after the spacecraft finally fall silent, they will keep coasting through interstellar space, and "analysis shows that they should remain intact," Perry said.

Scientists will still be able to calculate their positions based on their last known speed and trajectories, Weibel said. "We'll know intellectually where it is unless it gets intercepted."

An illustration of Voyager shown next to the Golden Record.

Theoretically, yes. But interstellar space is so unfathomably vast that the chances of aliens finding the probes are vanishingly small, Lee said.

That makes it unlikely that aliens will ever find the Voyager Golden Records — twin gold-plated discs meant to portray the diversity of life on Earth. They hold sounds such as wind and animal calls, greetings in 55 languages, images of our planet and a celestial map pinpointing its location — all intended for a hypothetical civilization capable of retrieving and playing them. (The probes also carry visual instructions for spinning the records).

In that sense, the Golden Records are as much an artistic exercise as a scientific one.

"The chances of us going after Voyager 1 and bringing it back for the Smithsonian — not that it's likely — are more believable than any chance of an alien civilization finding it, and then playing the record," Lee said.

Knowing that Voyager will continue its mission long past my lifetime actually gives me comfort

If an extraterrestrial civilization did find the probe after we lost contact with it, "we would have no way of knowing," Weibel added. "It becomes something that's powered entirely by imagination at that point."

So why send a message at all? All three scientists interviewed for this story see the Golden Record as a cosmic time capsule, a symbolic gesture meant to unite humanity around a shared home planet. For all its imperfections, including the omission of some widely spoken languages and a few mislabeled tracks, the disc remains an enduring emblem of human presence on Earth, they said.

"Knowing that Voyager will continue its mission long past my lifetime actually gives me comfort," Perry told Live Science. "Although I won't be able to speak to the Voyager spacecraft any more, I love knowing that I'll always be able to look to the stars and know they're still on their way."

Sharmila Kuthunur is an independent space journalist based in Bengaluru, India. Her work has also appeared in Scientific American, Science, Astronomy and Space.com, among other publications. She holds a master's degree in journalism from Northeastern University in Boston. Stablecoin Supply Shrinkage Raises a Bigger Question About Crypto Liquidity

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Crypto investors spend plenty of time staring at Bitcoin charts, but one of the more revealing signals this month may be the amount of dollar-backed stablecoins circulating across the market. Recent data suggests that the combined supply of major stablecoins has contracted materially from its earlier peak. That matters because these tokens are often the cash-like instruments traders use to move money between exchanges, DeFi protocols and new positions.

A shrinking supply does not automatically cause a market crash, and it certainly does not mean every stablecoin holder has left crypto. It does, however, raise a practical question: is the industry's readily deployable dollar liquidity getting smaller just as prices are falling?

CoinDesk's November 15 market report described the combined stablecoin market retreating toward roughly $244 billion, compared with a peak near $270 billion in May 2026. It also cited Glassnode data indicating a decline of approximately $17 billion over a one-month period. These are market snapshots from particular datasets, not a count of dollars sitting in every crypto trading account.

The distinction is significant. Stablecoin supply changes when issuers create or redeem tokens, but balances also shift among blockchains, venues and use cases. An on-chain transfer from one exchange to another does not shrink total issuance. A redemption in which tokens are destroyed can do so. Comparing data providers therefore requires consistent definitions and time windows.

Stablecoins serve several functions at once. They can be a temporary refuge during volatile markets, a settlement instrument for trading and a payments rail for companies that want digital dollars without holding Bitcoin or Ether. When aggregate supply grows, traders often interpret that as potential purchasing power entering the ecosystem. When it contracts, one plausible reading is that capital is being withdrawn or used elsewhere.

But supply and demand are not a one-step equation. A dollar token sitting idle in a wallet may be less important to near-term prices than a smaller amount actively circulating through exchanges. Conversely, a market can recover even while headline supply remains flat if existing balances change hands more frequently. The connection is useful, not deterministic.

The business case for stablecoins does not disappear when trading demand weakens. NewsBTC's coverage of Bitso Business's reported payment volumes illustrates how companies use digital dollars for transfers and settlement rather than speculation. Likewise, USDC's rollout through Samsung Wallet on Solana shows the payments and consumer-access dimension.

That creates an interesting tension. Trading-led demand can ebb even as infrastructure for real-world payments develops. Analysts should therefore ask where supply is shrinking, what users are doing with the remaining balances and whether dollar liquidity on exchanges is tightening faster than demand in payments networks.

Net issuance and redemptions from major issuers are an obvious starting point. Exchange stablecoin balances, cross-chain transfers and DeFi borrowing rates can help provide context, provided their methodology is clear. More importantly, these series should be compared with spot buying and ETF flows rather than used in isolation.

A return to stablecoin growth would not guarantee a rally, but it could indicate that more cash-like capacity is entering the system. Until then, the contraction is another reminder that crypto prices are shaped by the availability of capital as much as by the promise of the underlying technology.

InfoQ Homepage News Uber's Secure AI Framework Governs Autonomous AI Agents Across Their Lifecycle

Nov 15, 2026 3 min read

In a recent article, Uber Engineering highlights the limitations of traditional identity and API controls in securing AI agents that dynamically reason over external content and take real-world actions. To address this challenge, Uber developed a framework to determine whether an agent's action is appropriate given the user's request, the agent's intended purpose, its permissions, and the context of the interaction.

Identity and authorization systems can determine whether a user or service has permission to access a resource, but they can't determine whether an agent's proposed action is appropriate in context.

This creates multiple risks, including prompt injection, tool and memory poisoning, goal hijacking, behavioral drift, and hallucination-driven actions, which Uber's framework addresses through three layers spanning the entire agent lifecycle, from pre-deployment to runtime enforcement to post-deployment continuous assurance.

At provisioning time, agents must be registered in the Uber AI Registry, establishing identity, ownership, persona, permitted intents, constraints, dependencies, and risk classification. This process is automated using Argus, which analyzes the agent's source code, prompts, configurations, dependencies, exposed endpoints, and tool-invocation paths to identify security weaknesses. The same approach is enforced for MCP servers and tools used by the agents.

At runtime, the framework extends the traditional security model based on policy administration, policy decision, and policy enforcement to incorporate behavioral authorization, which evaluates whether an agent's actions are appropriate in a given context.

Behavioral authorization is implemented through three components: the AI Registry, AI guards, and Agent Guard runtime hooks. The AI Registry defines and manages agent policies, personas, intents, and constraints, while AI Guards evaluate interactions and proposed actions against established policies. Agent Guard runtime hooks invoke AI Guard "at critical checkpoints and applying its verdict before an interaction or action is allowed to proceed".

After deployment, the focus shifts to Continuous Assurance, which relies on end-to-end observability, auditing, explainability, and replay. This layer comprises two main main components: a unified execution trace that connects agent actions, tool calls, and security decisions; and recorded interactions that can be replayed whenever policies and guardrails are updated to assess their effectiveness in the new context.

Uber also identified several challenges in implementing security controls in production, including balancing policy restrictiveness to avoid both excessive false positives and insufficient protection, minimizing runtime latency, reducing developer friction during framework setup, and moving beyond generic controls towards agent-specific policies, which "made enforcement more contextual and precise".

Commenting on Uber's approach on LinkedIn, UN senior information technology officer Tristan G. raised several concerns, noting that while "per-agent personas, intents, constraints and calibration" is what adds the most value, they also come at a significant cost, as "the effort scales with the number of workflows". Additionally,

By its own account, the strongest control was restricting agents to explicit constraints, which is least privilege rather than a new idea. For me, the open question is how this works for agents you don't build, such as vendor or SaaS agents where you can't insert your own enforcement hooks.

On similar lines, AI architect Pratik Dhanave also observes that:

The lesson they call least glamorous is the one they rate most effective: explicit intents and constraints per agent. Not generic thresholds, which failed because every agent has a different purpose and risk profile. The cost is flexibility, and they say it is worth paying.

Uber's Secure AI framework is still in evolving, with plans to expand its capabilities across all the areas outlined above. There is much more to the framework than can be covered here, so readers are encouraged to consult the ⁠original article for a more comprehensive overview.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent says if Nvidia was a bank, ‘a lot of the executives would be removed’ — while Huang reportedly eyes return

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After blaming Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick for not blacklisting a company suspected of funneling AI chips to China, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent put Nvidia on blast.

The Treasury Department and other federal agencies were in favor of placing trade restrictions on Megaspeed, the cloud computing company at the center of Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

“Treasury, War, USTR, and State were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months,” Bessent told CNBC on Friday. “But Commerce has the pen.”

To help preserve the U.S. lead over China in the AI race, the Trump administration has blocked exports of certain high-end chips, including Nvidia’s, while allowing lesser models.

In an interview on Fox News’ Sunday Morning Futures, he was asked why Megaspeed wasn’t blacklisted. The Singapore-based company and its Malaysian subsidiary shipped at least $7.5 billion in Nvidia chips to China over two years, The Information reported.

Bessent pivoted away from the Commerce Department’s role and replied that what’s more important is “whether Nvidia had a culture of compliance around the U.S. export restrictions.”

He added that he also wants to know if compliance failures originated at a low level, somewhere in the middle, or up in the C-suite.

“These shell companies were obvious. They were being investigated, and did Nvidia use them as a workaround to sell chips to the Chinese?” Bessent asked. “And I think we’re going to see answers. There’s a strong independent lead director. They brought in outside counsel, and I think we’re going to get an explanation on this culture of non-compliance at Nvidia.”

On Thursday, Nvidia announced that Huang had stepped down as CEO. The company also named CFO Colette Kress as the interim CEO during the search for a replacement and appointed Harvey Jones as Nvidia’s first-ever board chair.

Kress is considered a possibility as a permanent CEO as well as Ian Buck, a top engineer at Nvidia. But outsiders like AMD CEO Lisa Su and Arm CEO Rene Haas are seen as potential candidates too.

Meanwhile, sources told the Financial Times that Huang has discussed with confidantes the possibility of returning to Nvidia, with one ally saying ‘war room’ of Silicon Valley executives is coming together to lobby for his return. Elon Musk and other executives are already circulating a public letter to bring back Huang.

Bessent said Sunday that he thinks Nvidia’s board should go outside the company for a new CEO, adding that it needs a “dramatic shakeup.”

While he called Nvidia a “wonderful company” and Huang an “incredible innovator,” that doesn’t absolve them of obligations to comply with export rules.

“I can tell you that if this were a bank and they hadn’t complied like this, the OCC would be in there right now and a lot of the executives would be removed,” Bessent said, referring to the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency.

He also insisted it doesn’t matter whether Alice Huang, who had a romantic affair with the former Nvidia CEO, is actually a spy for China.

Instead, Bessent turned the focus back on the culture at Nvidia and whether it used “seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings.”

Nvidia didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

For its part, Megaspeed said in a statement that it conducts legitimate and lawful business but has retained external legal counsel to conduct an internal investigation.

“Megaspeed designs and maintains its global operational and supply-chain control frameworks to comply with all applicable laws and regulatory regimes, including United States export control requirements and local jurisdiction rules in every market where we operate,” it said.

Megaspeed added that Alice Huang left the company effective Aug. 30, 2025 and currently has no role with there, while declining to comment on her personal and private matters.

Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing.

Sony is ditching its cross-product support app for dedicated alternatives

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Dedicated apps offer more control, but keeping track of multiple Sony products could become less convenient.

Sony will be discontinuing the Support by Sony app on February 28, 2027. The service support app distributed firmware update alerts, helped troubleshoot issues and even sent out company product and news notifications.

Instead of improving the "all-in-one" hub, the company will encourage users to download separate service apps for gadgets like the Sony WH-1000XM6 headphones, BRAVIA TVs and cameras. There's no argument over the fact that individual apps provide more control over compatible devices than the general-purpose app did. But if you own several different types of devices, this change makes managing your Sony products quite a hassle.

The Japanese giant has clarified that the app will be gone from Apple's App Store and Google Play after the expiration date. Thereafter, all existing app users will be greeted with a notice explaining that the service is no longer available. Users will receive no further firmware update messages, and even previously available product messages through the app will be inaccessible. However, you can still get all the support you need through Sony's dedicated apps and support center.

Sony is pointing everyone towards the Sony Sound Connect app for supported headphones and speakers. The app allows you to configure the equalizer settings and even install firmware updates on supported devices. The company revamped and renamed its existing Headphones Connect app to Sound Connect in October 2024, and it comes with extended support for several of its audio products, like the open earbuds and speakers of the LinkBuds line.

For TVs, the Sony BRAVIA Connect can handle remote control, setup and support for compatible TVs and soundbars like the Bravia Theater Bar 5. Meanwhile, Sony Creators' App gives owners of compatible cameras the ability to shoot remotely, transfer media and also receive firmware updates. The good part is that the apps allow users to directly interact with respective devices rather than just receive a ping about the pending updates.

But the BRAVIA Connect app doesn't work across all Sony TVs, and some older Sony cameras still need the Imaging Edge Mobile app. If you're among the brand-loyal Sony users, managing those devices could require some app-hopping going forward.

Now, even if your device is incompatible with these apps, you can still find manuals, look for available firmware downloads and get troubleshooting support through Sony's website. Some TV models running Android TV or Google TV also let you find software updates through the Settings or Help menu, depending on the model.

As far as bookmarks are concerned, Sony hasn't mentioned whether existing bookmarks will be transferred to another app. The company hasn't explained the reason for shutting down Support by Sony, an app that currently has more than 1 million downloads on Google Play.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Why personal finance isn't taught, and, how to teach it to kids

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In our school district, and most that I'm aware of, they added it as a required course right away. I'm surprised that if yours was truly one of the "top" that it wasn't already required, but it would have had to been integrated into the curriculum somewhere.

I don't think the course our kids take is particularly good, but it is better than nothing. They focus a lot on Ramsey. They play a lot of Ramsey videos and teaching. But, when they took the course, it became a nightly dinner conversation and we would go over what they learned and then I would provide sometimes a reinforcement or as many times an alternative and we would talk about it.
I asked her yesterday and she confirmed that she didn't have any courses. She agreed that our (my) interest drove her interest and knowledge.

I'm glad to hear that it's required now. I'd be interested to see what they're teaching; Ramsey has some good advice, but he can also be completely bonkers like suggesting an 8% SWR.

Some topics I'd like to see covered in no particular order:
  • Using a checkbook (our daughter was the only one in her post college rental house with a checkbook)
  • Budgeting on a $40k income or less, a reality for many college graduates today
  • Related, the impact of monthly subscriptions/fees
  • Filling out income tax forms
  • Navigating a pay stub (taxes, insurance, 401ks, etc.)
  • Compound interest and it's impact on savings/debt
Our daughter mentioned "financial advisors" coming in to her work to discuss how to invest in the 401k plan which was a waste for many at her research library who are living paycheck to paycheck.

Great topics. I'd probably argue against a few and maybe for many more.

On checkbook - my kids do not have checkbooks and see no reason to ever need them. There is virtually nothing anymore that requires a physical check. All money to them is digital. I do think talking about different forms of payment, security, costs/fees, and how to track and audit them in a digital world would be good.

Budgeting is a big discussion, but I'm not sure where you'd put that line. Instead, I think they should be teaching ROI on various career choices, including college. Remember this is HS level. On average, a 2 year technical degree or apprenticeship program will outperform a 4 year liberal arts degree (remember, I said on average), and kids should know that and know how to calculate the ROI and breakeven scenarios.

By the way, back to the original headline, 30 states now require a standalone high school financial literacy course to graduate. This is being taught in schools people.

it’s a bird, it’s a plane, it’s funny weather! by theo triantafyllidis

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

as extreme weather becomes harder to predict, this installation imagines a future in which the weather itself becomes the narrator of our lives.

annalise kamegawa November 15, 2026 7 min

In Theo Triantafyllidis’s video-based installation, Funny Weather, software-generated birds, planes, and clouds drift across a dreamy sky. At any point, the weather can turn gray, a flock can burst across the screen, or a kite can whip into view. To create this work, the artist draws on real-time data local to Manchester, where the installation is presented, to generate an uncanny AI-powered simulation of the world outside the exhibition space. Funny Weather, which premieres in mid-November at Aviva Studios in the UK, will travel to Hong Kong, Melbourne, and New York. In each new city, the work will draw on local data, creating a site-specific reflection of the weather in that location.

In conversation with designboom, Triantafyllidis revealed the APIs he worked with, the parallels between weather forecasting and divination, and how weather is becoming ‘the main character’. On first receiving the commission, which will travel between four international hosts, he notes, ‘I had this idea of a weather oracle or this strange weather prediction machine as a start.’

above and banner by Theo Triantafyllidis, preview still for Funny Weather, 2026 | image courtesy the ArtistTheo Triantafyllidis’s Funny Weather at Aviva Studios | image by Michael Pollard, courtesy of Factory International

To create Funny Weather, Triantafyllidis drew on three data sets. The first was the Open-Meteo API, which provides weather information: blue skies, overcast conditions, and impending thunderstorms. The next was a flight radar that reported which flights were passing over Aviva Studios. The last, which Triantafyllidis admits was the ‘most interesting’ to work with, was eBird and Merlin Bird ID – tools that track and identify bird sightings. The sightings are self-reported by users, making them a very subjective data set to work with. He notes that the data collected in eBird is ‘a way of digitizing nature that is very personal and strange. It has a bit of competitiveness to it and a bit of true love for what people are looking at.’

‘These are getting pulled daily and within intervals and fed into our custom system,’ says Triantafyllidis of how this data is translated into the visuals seen on screen. ‘With each of these three data streams, we’re [feeding] into different components of the system and driving different things.’

Although he admits that these data sets feel disparate, the artist says he is creating a kind of uncanny universe. He explains, ‘what I’m trying to do is give this data to a custom language model that we’re running and ask it to [prophesize] based on this data.’ Inside Funny Weather is what Factory International describes as a ‘bespoke locally hosted AI Oracle’ that generates the environment within the simulation. Although powered by the latest technology, the process reflects a kind of ancient logic. Triantafyllidis says it is ‘like looking back at how forecasting and divination worked in earlier human history, when maybe people were more in tune with weather and natural patterns and the general flow of things.’ He calls the task he gives the program a ‘nonsensical premise’ when considering the seemingly arbitrary selection of bird, weather, and flight-path data points. But by merging this information and transforming it into a visual landscape, he hopes that it will ‘tell us something useful and meaningful about our future.’

When talking about the installation at Aviva Studios, the artist notes that ‘sometimes the weather of our system aligns with what we see outside.’ He describes the lobby where the massive screen sits, continually morphing in response to Funny Weather and the data fed into it. ‘You can’t see the sky exactly,’ he says of the Manchester space, ‘but you can get the sense of what’s the weather outside. And here is a system [Funny Weather] attempting to align itself with the weather outside – sometimes successfully, sometimes not so successfully.’ The data itself is sometimes a bit wonky or inaccurate, causing what Triantafyllidis calls ‘stress’ in the environment. He says that sometimes there will be an influx of planes or ‘there [will be] birds that are not supposed to be there either – too early for the season or very unique, rare birds that are not seen in the area very frequently. This will affect the mood of the system.’

‘I think climate change is such a hyper object that is very tough for the human mind to comprehend,’ says Triantafyllidis on the urgency of the themes in his work. ‘We’ve reached a tipping point with the instability of the weather where we’re starting to experience really crazy shifts and weird meteorological phenomena happening on a very day-to-day basis and in every part of the world.’ He goes on to talk about the increased frequency of these extreme climate patterns and how, inevitably, the weather will become ‘this main character in our lives.’ Funny Weather acknowledges this inevitability: ‘I’m trying to imagine a good way to let the weather be the narrator of these stories, giving it this strange voice that can communicate somehow through this weird system.’

Theo Triantafyllidis’s Funny Weather at Aviva Studios | image by Michael Pollard, courtesy of Factory InternationalTheo Triantafyllidis at Aviva Studios | image by Michael Pollard, courtesy of Factory International

artist: Theo Triantafyllidis | @theo.trian

presented by: Factory International in collaboration with Australian Centre for the Moving Image, Onassis ONX, and M+

locations: Media Wall at Aviva Studios, home of Factory International, Manchester

dates: November 11–December 6, 2026, and January 19–March 26, 2027

Treyarch lays off lead Call of Duty writer and Zombies veteran after "approximately 20 years" of service

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"Thanks to all the fans for your enthusiasm, support, and kind words over the years."

Treyarch's former Senior lead writer, Craig Houston, has been laid off after approximately 20 years of service with the developer. In a post made to both his Twitter/X account and LinkedIn, the developer thanked fans for their support during his two decades with the company and confirmed he was impacted by recent reorganistation within the studio.

"After approximately 20 years, I was sadly one of those affected by recent layoffs," he noted in his posts. "I am proud to have worked with so many talented people - many of whom will remain friends for life. Thanks to all the fans for your enthusiasm, support, and kind words over the years."

On Twitter/X, he quote tweeted a video celebrating 30 years of Treyarch and featuring footage from many of the games he worked on to share his message.

Houston is a bonafide veteran of the esteemed FPS series. His first Call of Duty title at Treyarch was Call of Duty 3, where he worked as a video editor. This was followed by World at War, Black Ops 1 through 4, where he is credited as a writer and designer. For Modern Warfare, Black Ops - Cold War, Vanguard, Modern Warfare 3, then Black Ops 6 and 7, he is listed as a member of the design team and in 'creative development'. He is not credited on this year's Modern Warfare 4.

If you've picked up a Call of Duty game in the past decade-plus, you'll have interacted with Houston's work: he was a central creative force behind the writing for Treyarch's campaign modes since 2006 (with Call of Duty 3) and the main writer for the Zombies mode since 2008 (with Call of Duty: World at War). Alongside Jimmy Zielinski, Jason Blundell, and Kevin Drew, Houston was an integral part of the evolution of the Zombies pillar of Call of Duty, and shaped it into the series-defining thing it is today. There has been a fair amount of community support for Houston since he announced the news yesterday; he was a popular dev, and a lot of the Zombies-specific fan communities online hold a lot of respect for him.

"Craig is a great guy," notes one user on Reddit. "You can tell how much he loves the story/characters he writes just by how excited he is when he talks about them in promo material."

"You're a Legend Craig, it was an honor to work with you," says ex-Treyarch vet, Tony Flame, in a reply to Houston's X post. "You created a truly special experience that will live on in the kinds of millions fans. Wishing you the best [...]."

From all of us here at Eurogamer, and myself personally - who really admires the character-work and emergent storytelling present in the Xbox 360-era Call of Duty games, specifically - we wish you the best and good luck with the future, Craig.

Bryan Cranston & Aaron Paul Reunite For New Breaking Bad Release 13 Years After Finale

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's hard to believe it's been over 13 years since Breaking Bad's perfectly tragic series finale aired, bringing Vince Gilligan's television masterpiece to an epic conclusion in one of the best character-driven series ever written. Walter White's transformation into Heisenberg was a slow burn that lasted five seasons across the show's six-year run, while his partner in crime Jesse Pinkman went from a small-time meth dealer to one of television's most complex and sympathetic characters.

Thankfully, the finale wasn't quite the end of Gilligan's universe, with Better Call Saul taking us back to Albuquerque to see Jimmy McGill turn into the infamous Saul Goodman, while El Camino: A Breaking Bad Movie gave Jesse a much-needed epilogue following the original series' devastating conclusion. Both projects proved there were still plenty of compelling stories to tell in Gilligan's world.

Now, Bryan Cranston and Aaron Paul are officially returning to the Breaking Bad franchise this week alongside the rest of the original cast and even Vince Gilligan himself, with a brand-new release that revisits the legendary series that started it all.

Breaking Bad fans are in for a special reunion on November 17, 2026, with the release of Do What You're Gonna Do: The Definitive Oral History of Breaking Bad. The brand-new book brings together Bryan Cranston, Aaron Paul, and many of the original cast and crew for an extensive behind-the-scenes look at one of television's greatest dramas, more than 13 years after its finale.

Leading the project is Dean Norris, who famously portrayed DEA agent Hank Schrader, Walter White's brother-in-law and one of the show's most beloved characters. Co-written with bestselling author and journalist Joe Layden, the book combines Norris's own experiences on Breaking Bad with exclusive interviews, previously untold stories, and firsthand accounts from the people responsible for bringing the series to life.

You can order Do What You're Gonna Do: The Definitive Oral History of Breaking Bad via Amazon here.

The book effectively serves as a massive Breaking Bad reunion, featuring contributions from Cranston and Paul alongside series creator Vince Gilligan, Anna Gunn, Bob Odenkirk, and Giancarlo Esposito, as well as key creative figures like Peter Gould. It takes a look at the show's unlikely beginnings at AMC to its stratospheric rise and becoming a cultural phenomenon, with fascinating insights into how Gilligan's masterpiece came together.

Perhaps most poignantly, the book also features an interview with the late RJ Mitte, who played Walt Jr in the show. Mitte tragically passed away on October 12, 2026, at just 34 years old, making his contributions especially meaningful. An excerpt published by Vanity Fair on November 10 revealed that Mitte reflected on his original audition, his experiences portraying Walt Jr., and some of the show's most heartbreaking moments. Norris also pays tribute to his former costar, making the book a touching celebration of the cast's enduring friendships.

​​​​​​​Do What You're Gonna Do officially releases this Tuesday, November 17 through William Morrow, with hardcover, digital, and audiobook editions available at most major distributors. Norris also narrates the audiobook, giving fans a way to hear directly from the actor behind the legendary Hank Schrader.

While it isn't exactly an epic on-screen reunion, bringing together so many familiar voices to celebrate Breaking Bad makes this a truly exciting release for longtime fans, and one that gives us a look behind the scenes at one of the greatest series in modern television.​​​​​​​

Lancer la V1 de son application mobile en 2 semaines : la méthode en 4 étapes

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Beaucoup de porteurs de projet pensent qu'une application mobile demande six mois de travail avant de voir le jour. Dans la réalité, la plupart des projets peuvent sortir une première version utilisable en deux semaines environ. La condition : savoir ce que l'on construit, et surtout ce que l'on ne construit pas encore.

Chez La Belle App, agence de développement d'applications mobiles et web sur mesure, nous avons lancé plus de 80 apps en 5 ans. Voici la méthode que nous suivons sur chaque projet.

La première erreur est de vouloir tout mettre dans la V1. Le cadrage sert à répondre à trois questions :

Une app de réservation, par exemple, n'a pas besoin d'un programme de fidélité au lancement. Elle a besoin qu'un client trouve un créneau, le réserve et reçoive une confirmation. Tout le reste vient après les premiers retours.

Avant d'écrire une ligne de code, nous concevons les écrans sur Figma et nous les partageons avec le client. C'est l'étape la moins chère pour changer d'avis : déplacer un bouton sur une maquette prend une minute, le faire dans une app déjà développée peut prendre une journée.

Les maquettes servent aussi à montrer le projet à de futurs utilisateurs, à des associés ou à des investisseurs. Elles transforment une idée floue en quelque chose de concret.

Pour aller vite sans sacrifier la qualité, nous privilégions Flutter. Une seule base de code produit une app iOS et une app Android avec une expérience fluide sur les deux. Concrètement, cela divise presque par deux le temps de développement et le coût de maintenance par rapport à deux apps natives séparées.

La version test est installée sur le téléphone du client avant la publication. C'est le moment de vérifier les parcours réels, les textes, les notifications, les paiements.

La publication sur l'App Store et Google Play a ses propres règles : captures d'écran, description, politique de confidentialité, comptes de test pour les équipes de revue d'Apple. Anticiper ces éléments dès le cadrage évite de perdre une semaine au moment de la sortie.

La technique compte, mais ce qui accélère un projet est souvent ailleurs :

Les outils no-code et les solutions toutes faites conviennent pour tester une idée très simple. Dès que l'app devient le cœur d'un business (paiement, réservation, mise en relation, données propres), le sur mesure reprend l'avantage : pas de limite imposée par un éditeur, pas d'abonnement qui grimpe avec le nombre d'utilisateurs, et une app qui vous appartient.

Une V1 rapide n'est pas une question de vitesse de frappe. C'est une question de choix : un parcours clair, des maquettes validées, une technologie adaptée et une sortie sur les stores préparée dès le départ.

Vous avez un projet d'application ? Vous pouvez en parler avec notre équipe sur labelleapp.com.

Billel Loukit, cofondateur de La Belle App

How to check if an email address is real without sending an email

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sooner or later every app that collects email addresses needs to know whether an address is real. You can get a surprisingly long way without sending a single message. This guide builds four checks in Node, from the cheapest to the most invasive, and it is honest about where each one stops being reliable.

All the snippets are ES modules, so save them as .mjs files and run them on a current Node release.

Skip the giant regex. The full email grammar is enormous and almost nobody needs it. Check the few rules that catch real mistakes: exactly one @, a sensible length, no spaces or brackets in the name, and a domain with at least one dot.

This accepts someone@gmail.com and rejects name@@gmail.com, name@gmail and anything with a space in it. It does not prove the mailbox exists. It only proves the text is shaped like an address.

An address can be perfectly formatted and still point nowhere. DNS tells you where a domain receives mail, through MX records, and Node ships the lookup in node:dns/promises.

Two details matter here. Lower priority numbers are tried first, so the list is sorted by them. And a domain with no MX records is not automatically dead: RFC 5321 says a sender should fall back to the domain's A or AAAA record, which is called an implicit MX. A domain that publishes a single null MX has said it accepts no email at all, so the function returns an empty list for it.

Run it against gmail.com and you get back a short list of mail hosts. Run it against a made-up domain and you get an empty list.

A throwaway inbox passes every check above, because its domain is real and has a mail server. A maintained public list of disposable domains, kept as a plain text file with one domain per line, lets you flag them. Download it on a schedule rather than copying it once, because the list keeps growing.

The loop also matches parent domains, because a service often hands out addresses on many subdomains. Treat a match as a flag, not a verdict. Some people use a throwaway address for ordinary privacy reasons, so decide for your own product whether to block it or just warn.

The last step is to ask the destination server whether it would accept mail for the address. You connect to a host from the MX lookup, say hello, name a sender, and then name the recipient with RCPT TO. A 250 reply means the server would take the message. A 550 means there is no such mailbox. You then say QUIT before any message body is sent, so nothing is delivered.

Use helo and mailFrom values from a domain you control, and try the function on a mail server you run yourself while you are learning. It is less reliable against the open internet than it looks:

So treat the result as three buckets, accepted, rejected and unknown, and be comfortable with the third one.

The first three checks are cheap, and they are worth keeping in your own code. The probe is where the ongoing work lives: access to port 25, retries for greylisting, catch-all detection and IP reputation all need care over time.

If you would rather not run that yourself, a hosted email verification service does the probing for you. There are plenty to choose from and many have a free tier. Compare them on how they treat catch-all domains and how often they answer unknown, because those are the cases a plain 250 cannot settle.

Disclosure: I work on Emailchaser, which offers one of these, a free email checker. It runs 3 free checks a day with no signup, and it marks a catch-all domain as risky rather than proven, for the reasons above.

This post was drafted with AI assistance, and the code samples were run and tested before publishing.
