Robert Christgau and Carola Dibbell Talk New Documentary ‘The Last Critic’ and Staying Crazy in Love

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Last Critic is one of the year’s most fascinating rock documentaries — even though it’s not about a musician. It’s a portrait of the legendary writer Robert Christgau, the self-proclaimed “Dean of American Rock Critics.” He was one of the pioneers who invented pop-music criticism in the Sixties — but six decades later, at 83, he’s still at the top of the game. He began his Consumer Guide column in the Village Voice in 1969, and has kept cranking it out ever since, now on Substack. For generations of music fanatics, Christgau’s voice hits like Bob Dylan or Neil Young — two artists his age who are still doing it with the same fiercely independent spirit he brings to his writing.

The debut feature from director Matty Wishnow, The Last Critic won the jury prize at the South by Southwest and Oak Cliff Film festivals, as well as the 2026 Screen Dallas grand prize. It gets its New York premiere tomorrow. The film, which is co-produced by longtime Rolling Stone contributor and editor Joe Levy, goes beyond music, because it’s also the portrait of a marriage. Christgau and his wife, novelist Carola Dibbell, have been together more than 50 years. Over the decades, they’ve shared an intellectual life, raised their daughter, collected way too much vinyl, and — the weirdest part — remained crazy in love.

Christgau has a fearsome rep for never pulling his punches, with his own savage wit. Lou Reed spent a chunk of his live album Take No Prisoners complaining about getting a B+ grade for Street Hassle; when U2 won the Grammy Award for Album of the Year in 1988 for The Joshua Tree, Bono jokily groused in his speech about his band’s reviews in the Village Voice. Christgau’s inspired more songs attacking him than any other journalist, from Sonic Youth (“Kill Yr Idols”) to Public Enemy (“Bring the Noise”).

But there’s something obsessive about his lifelong quest to chronicle the music, still going strong after 60 years — he’s heard more albums than anyone on the planet. When he reviewed Prince’s Dirty Mind in 1980, he dropped one of the most-quoted one-liners in critical history: “Mick Jagger can fold up his penis and go home.” Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

The Last Critic features artists Christgau has reviewed, as well as writers he’s inspired, from Sonic Youth’s Thurston Moore to Pulitzer-winning novelist Colson Whitehead. (I make an appearance as well.) There’s a touching interlude with his lifelong friend Greil Marcus, the only other OG rock critic who’s kept doing it this long (or this well). In one of the most surprising moments, the legendary songwriter Randy Newman recalls their friendship, and admits he wrote a song about Christgau and Dibbell, “Lost Without You,” inspired by the closeness of their marriage. It’s the kind of movie where you come for the rock, stay for the romance.

Rolling Stone met up with the couple at the same East Village pad they’ve called home for 50 years. The place is packed with albums, books, art — the first thing that greets you at the door is a painting of the Shirelles. They talked about the doc, seeing their marriage up on the big screen, the art of arguing, playing hoops with Randy Newman, and the strange college semester when Christgau was Gene Simmons’ professor.

Congratulations on the movie.Robert Christgau: It came out really well. When [director] Matty [Wishlow] called me up and asked if I would do it, the first thing I said was, “Yes, if you include my marriage,” which at the time I think took him aback. However, he came to feel that it was the marriage that made the movie, which is what I believe.

It is the marriage that makes the movie.Carola Dibbell: But it’s still about you. I’m a co-star. Related Content Andrew Watt on His Insane 2026:  Madonna, McCartney, the Stones, Gaga  The Rolling Stones' Release Date for ‘Foreign Tongues’ Deluxe Edition Look for the Legends in the Best Rock Album Race at 2027 Grammys Alexander Skarsgård Embraces His Magnetism in the Rolling Stones’ Video for ‘Mr. Charm’

When I first saw it, I told the director, “This is a love story. A very strange love story.”Christgau: Well, I agree. I think that’s the best thing about the movie. The relationship really has a kind of vitality to it that you can see. And that’s exactly how I feel about Carola, and that’s the most important thing in my life. And it’s really nice that it actually comes through in the imagery of the film. I think that’s the reason people like the movie, too.

When did you two get together?Dibbell: The summer of 1972. Bob and I had known each other. He was my friend Dominique’s boyfriend. Bob had had his eye on me all this time, but I was just so delighted to have him as a friend. I knew that he was a rock critic, but I really was not like a groupie. I met him in the summer of 1970 — we were all having breakfast at Ratner’s Deli. But I then got into an argument with you about Godard.

Christgau: She knew more about film than I did.

Dibbell: Bob and I had a big argument about it, but then we became friends anyway, and then he began to date Dominique, and so we just knew each other. But eventually, everything changed for me.

Christgau: I’m so glad it did, Carola. She was an incredibly cute woman with a Radcliffe degree.

Dibbell: The thing is we were both English majors. One night we were playing a party game called “Celebrity,” and we needed to come up with a celebrity whose initials were an M and a C. And we both came up with Martin Chuzzlewit [the title character in an 1844 Dickens novel].

Now that’s two people destined to be together. I think that’s why people respond seeing such an unusual couple onscreen.Christgau: What makes it so unusual?

Two people, very extreme personalities, who stay madly in love for 50 years of marriage, but remain individuals in a totally unfiltered way.Christgau: I don’t know how extreme I think we are, but—

Dibbell: Really? I really don’t know that, but I believe you.

You both make a strong impression. Let me put it that way.Dibbell: Well, at the time that we got together, it was … I’m not going to say “turning point,” but it was sort of a point where one chapter in the counterculture and the movement was easing into another. People were asking, “Oh, is it OK to be a feminist and get married?” Bob was a feminist too, and he always liked smart women. And so we both were trying to figure out, how do you do this? A marriage of equals?

Something I love about the documentary is how it captures your enthusiasm for the art of a good argument. Dibbell: I used to say that if I told him “I agree with you,” he’d say, “That’s where you’re wrong.” He just couldn’t stop arguing.

Dibbell: Yeah, you. Who do you think we’re talking about? But it just comes very easily to both of us.

Would it be fair to say that you both have argumentative personalities?Christgau: I don’t think Carola has an argumentative personality.

Dibbell: There’s where I disagree with you.

I guess I mean a “debate” more than an “argument.”Dibbell: I think I argue more than I debate, actually. But it definitely improves our thought to be arguing together.

Christgau: I got Carola to start writing rock criticism, not because she was my wife and I wanted to give her the work — although, obviously, that was part of it. But because A, I knew she could really write, and B, she had knowledge of the subject.

Dibbell: I found rock criticism so interesting as a form, being a writer, and I was very interested in the unreliable narrator and all that kind of thing. Then when I read Lester Bangs, I said, “Oh, yeah! Why not be literally ambitious while writing about this declassé music stuff?”

Christgau: He could also write a paragraph in about a minute and a half.

When you began writing in the Sixties, there wasn’t a lot of serious attention paid to pop music.Christgau: But Rolling Stone was doing that a lot. Rolling Stone was a crucial part of why all of that happened. Jann Wenner had a head for finding the most interesting writers, many from the Boston college scene, and that was a really important factor in why I got to do what I did. No question about it.

And also the fact that I was already friends with Greil Marcus. That was a crucial part of it as well. He was at Rolling Stone, and frankly, I think he had Jann Wenner around his little finger. Because Jann was smart enough to know that this guy was really something else.

Early on, you wrote that your biggest influences were Thelonius Monk and Alan Freed.Christgau: Let me tell you, Monk has lasted. I still play a lot of Thelonious Monk. Carola gets a little tired of it.

Dibbell: I finally say, “Can we listen to The Clash?”

But you always had an appreciation for AM radio. You were open to tawdry pop trash.Christgau: It wasn’t “tawdry.” Come on.

You were writing in the Seventies about how great KC and the Sunshine Band were. In the Village Voice.Christgau: It’s all well-made, that’s the thing. Extremely well-made. That proves my point.Dibbell: It proves your point.

Determined as you are to undermine the entire premise of my point, I’m going to make it anyway. When I first read your Consumer Guide as a kid in the Eighties, I couldn’t believe there was an adult writing about Funkadelic and the Go-Go’s and Donna Summer and A Flock of Seagulls and taking it seriously, as art.

Christgau: Four hours a day listening to AM radio had something to do with that. But I wanted to write about pop art as an important phenomenon.

There’s a great story in your memoir about when you were teaching the rock criticism class at CUNY on Staten Island in 1970, and one of your students was a kid named Gene Klein.Christgau: Who later became Gene Simmons.

I always wonder what the dragon from Kiss learned from you about music critics.

Christgau: He just sat in the back and kept his mouth shut.

Something The Last Critic documents is your insanely obsessive online following, around the world.Christgau: One thing that happens with this movie is people in Sweden and Norway coming up with their dog-eared copies of my books.

Dibbell: It was so funny hearing about Europeans with tattered versions of the first Consumer Guide book.

Christgau: So I had this fanbase all over the world, and I had no idea it was there, really. I was writing for the Village Voice, and they paid me decently, and I had a lot of autonomy, and I really valued it.  But when that ended, I kept writing. I managed to have a whole life as a critic, and it’s not like I’m working for Variety or the Times. I feel not just fortunate, I feel like I built something.

Dibbell: But there was a point when you were having conversations about whether you should stick with the Consumer Guide or whether you should write more essays. Also, I remember I had to argue Bob into writing the memoir, Going Into the City. You felt, “Why would anybody want to read my story?”

Christgau: Which came out pretty well, I think.

Dibbell: Yeah, it did. But you resisted that. Editors thought you should write more essays, but I felt that the Consumer Guide was such a great form that you shouldn’t stop doing it. And I also understood that you had a fanbase based on that. I wasn’t even thinking so you can make a living — but just with me, my constant struggle to get a book published, I just felt this is a great thing, that fanbase. You’re very lucky that people want to be your fan.

How did you become friends with Randy Newman? He plays such a surprising role in the movie. Dibbell: He was lovely.

Randy Newman talks about writing a beautiful love song about you two, “Lost Without You.”Christgau: I will say I was very proud. I wrote about Randy in the very beginning, and I felt that Randy really did understand that I got him. I was early on it. I was initially assigned by this guy from BMI named Russ Sanchez when I was writing for the BMI magazine who told me about this songwriter guy named Randy Newman who they wanted to have a piece on. At that time he lived in a house somewhere in the Valley, so I went there and I interviewed him. And then we went out to the ballpark and played basketball. This was a long time ago — I don’t play basketball anymore. But we had a game of basketball.

Dibbell: Don’t you each remember that the other one won?

Christgau: Well, he was taller than me. But I do rebound with a certain amount of verve.

It started with basketball, but how did that turn into a long-running friendship?Dibbell: He wrote you a fan letter in the Nineties, saying that he admired your perseverance, the way you kept up with new music, that it was something he did not actually understand, your enthusiasm for it. But he was interested in hip-hop and he wanted you to guide him. He wanted you to recommend him some hip-hop.

What did you play him?Dibbell: Public Enemy, I think.

Christgau: I’ve always had a high opinion of him.

Dibbell: We had lunch with him — this was in the old days when we had an answering machine. We got a message from someone claiming to be Randy Newman, and I said, “Oh, it’s not Randy Newman.” But you got back to him and said, “Was that you?” And he said, “Yes.” You were both old by then.

Christgau: We went to lunch. Then he came back to our place, even though he could hardly walk.

Dibbell: He had some bad knee problem, but you wanted to play him some new music. He climbed the stairs. Then he looked around our apartment, and he said, “If you wanted to fix this place up, I could loan you some money.”

He’s so sincere in this movie — we’ve never seen this side of him before. It’s probably the only Randy Newman song anyone would want to be about them.Christgau: Yeah, it’s a great compliment. I liked that song before I knew it was about us.

Were you surprised by The Last Critic, when you finally saw it?Christgau: I think a better way of putting it is that I was more pleased by it than I anticipated being. And I was especially happy about how important a role Carola played in it. Matty hadn’t made a movie before. This was not his area of expertise. But he ended up making a really good movie.

It just seemed like, “OK, you want to do this? What do you see in a documentary about a critic?”

But it goes beyond that. It’s the love story, in a collaborative life of the mind.Christgau: That is the thing that I like best about it, and I’m proud of it. Obviously, I’m very happy who it was a love story with. If it had been about [the legendary New Yorker and Village Voice journalist] Ellen Willis [his Sixties ex] and me, it wouldn’t have been this good. Ellen’s as smart as anybody I’ve ever met. But Ellen didn’t like music as much as Carola ended up doing. [Dibbell comes in from the next room.] I was just saying you were a better rock critic than Ellen Willis.

Dibbell: I respect her tremendously. Her work was foundational.

You were on the scene for so many pivotal rock moments, like the Monterey Pop festival and Woodstock. A fan on your Substack recently found a photo of you in the crowd at Monterey. Christgau: About the third row, yeah. Nobody at that time understood how important Monterey was going to turn out to be historically, as this event that really just pretended something was happening — but then it really happened. Woodstock itself was a strange and interesting experience, which involved a lot of lying around the grass, among other things.

The way you wrote about women musicians in the 1970s was very ahead of the curve culturally. You were engaging with feminist ideas at a time when it was rare in music journalism. There’s a really moving moment in the doc when the writer Jessica Hopper discusses reading your words on Joni Mitchell.Christgau: Let it be said that Ellen Willis was not without her presence in that fact, because Ellen Willis really made me a much more fervent and conscience-driven and militant feminist. It was really important to me to be a feminist critic. I mean, I had those impulses anyway, but Willis obviously solidified them, without any question. I’ve been writing about women making rock & roll since the beginning. Joy of Cooking and Fanny are bands that 90 percent of people have never heard of.

You were taking Dolly Parton seriously as a writer in the 1970s, when most of the media treated her as a joke.Christgau: But Patti Smith was out there covering “Jolene,” right? Glad she’s still around. How old is Patti?

Dibbell: She’s a little younger than us, but not that much younger than us. So, what, she’s in her seventies?

She turns 80 in December.Dibbell: And Debbie Harry? I think she’s older than me.

You had a great line about a Billy Joel album in the Eighties: “Sexual-politics rads like me were fretting about a decade ago come home to haunt guys who thought they were a crock.”Christgau: I said that? That’s pretty good. But as far as my marriage with Carola is concerned, one thing that’s really significant about how it happened is that I went out with one of her best friends, for close to a year before. And so Carola was someone I really knew fairly well, but it wasn’t like we were dating.

Dibbell: Not even a little bit. I was really not ready to get married again in that time. I’d been married before — I got married on my way to get divorced. I was not ready for a potential husband, so I was very happy to know Bob as a friend.

As you said before, you were figuring out a new way of doing this — a marriage of equals. Dibbell: Well, I had to figure out how to be heard, when you were surrounded by people who were just going to listen to you. I had to figure out how to do that. Despite all the struggling to be an equal and all that, I do spend a lot of time feeling like Bob is doing what he’s doing, and I’m helping make it work. But at the same time, I mean, when I finally published a novel [The Other Ones, in 2014] and had readings and did events as myself, it was very different. And Bob was great. He came as my date on an event I did at Rochester [New York]. He said “I love being the date. I love not having to do the work.”

Some of the best moments in the doc are just you two geeking out about music together.Christgau: There was an emotional weight to it that most documentaries about critics wouldn’t have. I just understood that the marriage was integral to the fact that I did this work, and therefore it had to be there.

Dibbell: I believe Bob when he says that, but I don’t necessarily experience it that way. I mean, I rarely talk you out of an opinion about a record.

Dibbell: I mean, I really was amazed by how much of this movie was about work, judgment, criticism. So there are all these shots where he’s writing at his computer, with all these piles of books collapsing around him. And he’s sitting there thinking, “Is this an A or an A-minus?” But one of my favorite scenes is when I’m editing Bob. Just because it seemed so relaxed and there were so many things we agreed about without really having to spell them out. And I like that. I like that a lot.

I’m just so used to this setup — our marriage, how we interact with each other — I don’t even think about how it works. There’s just so much that we share. I’m so used to it. Trending Stories 'SNL' Cold Open: Jensen Huang, Tech CEOs Get a Crash Course in Female Spies 'SNL' Weekend Update Roasts Trump Admin Over Grounded Flights, Jensen Huang Affairs Could 'Rubber Soul' Be the Best Beatles Album? (Yes) Lana Del Rey, Dua Lipa, Zara Larsson Contribute New Music to 'GTA VI: The Album'

Christgau: After half a century, I would hope you’re used to it.

Meglio di Allegri e Fonseca, non di Pioli: i numeri delle prime 11 in A di Amorim al Milan

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il Milan lavora in questa sosta in attesa del rientro dei giocatori in giro per il mondo con le proprie nazionali: alla ripartenza l'obiettivo sarà quello di trovare la continuità mancata fino a qui, dopo un inizio di stagione fatto di alti (come il derby vinto) e bassi (soprattutto in Europa). I numeri al contempo dicono che le prime undici di campionato sorridono non poco al nuovo tecnico, Ruben Amorim.

Se mettiamo a confronto i dati statistici relativi alle prime undici giornate di campionato dell'attuale rendimento del Milan guidato da Amorim con i precedenti degli ultimi anni sulla panchina rossonera, emerge che il portoghese sta portando la squadra sulla via corretta. Al termine dell'undicesimo turno della stagione in corso, la squadra ha totalizzato 24 punti, occupando la quarta posizione in classifica a cinque lunghezze di distanza dalla Lazio capolista, che è a quota 29.

Dodici mesi fa, nello stesso punto del campionato, la formazione allenata allora da Allegri aveva raccolto due punti in meno, fermandosi a quota 22 dopo il pareggio esterno per 2-2 ottenuto contro il Parma. In quella circostanza, tuttavia, la graduatoria vedeva i rossoneri al terzo posto, appaiati al Napoli e staccati di sole due lunghezze dalla coppia di testa composta da Roma e Inter.

Allargando il confronto alle annate precedenti, per individuare una partenza superiore rispetto ai 24 punti attuali è necessario risalire alla stagione 2022, il penultimo campionato con Pioli alla guida tecnica, quando il club raccolse 26 punti nelle prime undici gare, cinque in meno rispetto all'annata della conquista dello Scudetto. Successivamente, i numeri dopo undici giornate hanno registrato i 22 punti nell'ultima gestione Pioli e i 17 punti raccolti durante la parentesi di Fonseca, confermando la crescita numerica rispetto alle ultime tre stagioni.

Inter-Roma... finisce 6-4. L'analisi in 10 punti della "nostra" corsa scudetto

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Non ce ne vogliano Lazio, Milan e Como. Ma oggi, per quel che ha mostrato fino a questo momento la Serie A, la sensazione è che nel momento caldo della stagione la corsa Scudetto possa ridursi a due sole contendenti. Inter e Roma. Per qualità della rosa, gioco espresso, attitudine al successo e interpretazioni dei singoli, ad oggi sono loro due le nostre candidate allo Scudetto con la possibilità di essere smentiti sul campo nelle prossime settimane. Fatta la doverosa premessa, eccoci qua al gioco che ci siamo proposti nella redazione di TuttoMercatoWeb.

- Portieri (ROMA) Pepo Martinez, Provedel e Di Gennaro da una parte. Svilar, Gollini e Vasquez dall'altra. Parte tutto da qui. La sfida a distanza fra Inter e Roma vede passare subito in vantaggio i giallorossi che con Svilar hanno probabilmente il miglior numero uno dell'intero campionato. Dopo la sosta rientrerà, dando un ulteriore boost di qualità alla rosa di Gasperini.

- Difensori (INTER) Il pareggio dell'Inter arriva presto. Qua almeno sulla carta non c'è storia, con i nerazzurri che per qualità ed esperienza ad alti livelli sono sopra ai giallorossi. Con Bisseck, Bastoni, Stones, Akanji, Pavard più il jolly Carlos Augusto, in pochi possono reggere il confronto a livello italiano. Non che la Roma sia messa male con Mancini, Ndicka ed Hermoso più il supporto di Balerdi, Ghilardi e Kourielakis, ma l'Inter vince questo confronto.

- Centrocampisti (INTER) Anche qua primeggia l'Inter. Per centrocampo si intendono i centrali ma pure gli esterni: Dimarco e lo Spence delle ultime uscite sono al top nel ruolo, anche se il confronto con Wesley e Molina non è così impari. Nel mezzo il top della Roma è senza dubbio Manu Kone, l'Inter risponde con i vari Calhanoglu, Barella, Zielinski, Sucic e via dicendo. Nel complesso meglio il reparto di Chivu.

- Attaccanti (INTER) La Roma ha il giocatore che ha segnato di più da inizio 2026 ad oggi. Ma l'Inter ha un pacchetto attaccanti ben più completo. Thuram e Lautaro ovviamente. Più Pio Esposito e Bonny. In Europa poche squadre possono permettersi tanta abbondanza tecnica. La Roma è strutturata diversamente, con Malen cannibale di gol e minuti e Dybala che quando sta bene è fra i migliori della Serie A. Quando sta bene, appunto. Vince l'Inter, ancora.

- Panchina (INTER) Parliamo di profondità della rosa, di possibilità di ruotare i titolari senza abbassare la qualità e di incisività a gara in corso con le sostituzioni. E anche qua ha la meglio Chivu, con scelte top in tutti i reparti, soprattutto a centrocampo. Ma anche le riserve, o presunte tali, di difesa e attacco sono di primissimo livello. Punto su cui alla Roma forse manca qualcosa.

- Allenatore (ROMA) Non ce ne voglia Chivu che ha vinto con grande merito l'ultimo scudetto. Ma il Gasperini che si sta vedendo in questa stagione a Roma sembra avere qualcosa in più, sorretto anche da una spinta motivazionale che potrebbe dare quel quid in più nel momento caldo della stagione.

- Ambiente e tifosi (ROMA) Questo è il punto probabilmente più controverso. O combattuto per meglio dire. L'Olimpico di parte giallorossa continua a macinare sold out ma pure il San Siro nerazzurro è contesto che spinge e regala sostegno. E allora a fare la differenza potrebbero essere le aspettative: perché dall'Inter strafavorita secondo tutti ci si aspetta quasi che debba vincere. Mentre per la Roma, non dovesse arrivare lo Scudetto, non sarebbe certamente un fallimento. E poi c'è l'entusiasmo: col passare delle giornate, quello di fede romanista potrebbe dare quel boost in più alla squadra di Gasperini.

- Società (INTER) Qua vince l'Inter per un ragionamento di struttura e continuità. Il tutto fa riferimento a Beppe Marotta, probabilmente il dirigente migliore che oggi il calcio italiano ha da offrire. La presenza dei Friedkin e il loro coinvolgimento nella Roma sono sempre più forti col passare delle stagioni, ma il comparto dirigenziale dell'Inter (anche con l'addio di Ausilio) resta fra i migliori a livello italiano. E non solo.

- Disponibilità sul mercato di gennaio (ROMA) Qua potrebbe spuntarla la Roma, con il fortissimo coinvolgimento di Gian Piero Gasperini nelle scelte dei rinforzi. Uno, forse due, arriveranno sicuramente. In casa Inter invece valutazioni ancora in corso, ma la possibilità che in inverno non saranno fatti nuovi acquisti esiste. Da qui il vantaggio dei giallorossi.

- Mentalità e abitudine al successo (INTER) Cose che non si possono insegnare e che si apprendono solo col tempo. L'Inter è al vertice in Italia e in Europa da anni, ecco perché è avanti rispetto alla Roma in questo singolo aspetto. Questioni, mentalità e abitudine alla vittoria, che a fine stagione potrebbero avere un peso importante.

- Il peso della Champions Qua le variabili sono tante. E si parte dal capire se Inter e Roma andranno avanti oltre la League Phase, di cui mancano ancora 4 partite. Ma sono discorsi prematuri, nella speranza che entrambe possano superare senza playoff questo primo turno di Champions League.

21 Financial Institutions Plan to Launch a New Dollar Stablecoin in 2027. Who Will Be the Biggest Winners and Losers?

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investors now have an expanding array of options for investing in the $300 billion stablecoin industry.

The $300 billion stablecoin industry continues to see an influx of new entrants. There are now 10 different stablecoins with market caps of $1 billion or higher, led by the two stablecoin giants Tether (USDT +0.02%) and USDC (USDC +0.01%).

The latest entrant -- and one of the most intriguing ones to date -- is known as Project Faro. It's backed by 21 different banks and financial institutions, and has already raised $270 million for a new stablecoin launch in 2027.

So what does Project Faro mean for investors, and will it affect how they invest in the rapidly growing stablecoin industry?

To answer that question, it's important to know some of the heavy hitters involved in Project Faro. Wall Street investment bank Goldman Sachs (GS +0.32%) is playing a key role, as is Citigroup (C -0.04%). Other financial institutions involved in the launch include Wells Fargo (WFC +0.34%), Deutsche Bank (DB -0.08%), and Fidelity.

While Project Faro is still in the preliminary launch phase, the focus appears to extend far beyond just launching a U.S. dollar stablecoin. Plans also call for launching stablecoins pegged to other G7 currencies, including a stablecoin pegged to the euro. So this new stablecoin venture has truly worldwide ambitions.

If that sounds familiar, that's because it is. In June, a consortium of 140 different financial institutions and fintech companies announced the launch of a new stablecoin called Open USD (OUSD -0.08%). This dollar-pegged stablecoin already has a market cap of $1 billion, and has the support of some big names as well, including BlackRock (BLK +0.52%), Visa (V +1.30%), and Mastercard (MA +1.32%).

From my perspective, this is setting up an epic battle for stablecoin dominance. Right now, the two leaders are Tether and USDC, and it's not even close. Together, they account for roughly 80% of the total stablecoin market. But the arrival of these massive new financial consortia -- backed by dozens of banks and mainstream financial institutions -- can't be ignored.

The company most at risk here is Circle Internet Group (CRCL -2.54%), the issuer of the USDC stablecoin. The higher the global supply of USDC is, the more money Circle makes. Thus, the arrival of new deep-pocketed rivals could put a serious dent in Circle's future growth prospects.

Companies attempting to go it alone in the stablecoin race could be left behind. For that reason, I'm also concerned about PayPal (PYPL +1.68%). The company has a rapidly growing stablecoin, PayPal USD (PYUSD -0.02%), but is not part of either financial consortium.

The biggest winners could be Mastercard and Visa. They have done the most to move their payments to blockchain rails, and are actively working to make stablecoin-backed payments a reality. Earlier this year, for example, Mastercard launched a massive new Crypto Partner Program that involves more than 100 blockchain, fintech, and crypto firms.

It remains to be seen how these new stablecoin initiatives will affect crypto-native companies. The one company that I'm watching closely now is Coinbase (COIN -2.31%). It helped to launch the USDC stablecoin in partnership with Circle in 2018, and is now one of the founding partners of the Open USD consortium.

Both banks and credit card issuers are clearly responding to the threat posed by stablecoins. If stablecoins are able to offer highly attractive yields, they might be able to siphon away traditional bank deposits. If stablecoins become a mainstream form of payment, they might pose a threat to the big credit card giants.

That's one reason why there was so much debate over the Digital Asset Market Clarity Act ("Clarity Act") earlier this year. Banks and traditional financial institutions are doing everything they can to protect themselves against the rise of new rivals. From this perspective, both Project Faro and Open USD can be seen as defensive reactions to the exploding popularity of stablecoins.

The good news, if you're a crypto investor, is that there are now new ways to play the stablecoin boom. You can now invest in banks and financial institutions, not just fintech firms and crypto-native companies. Some early winners are already starting to emerge, and could be worth a closer look.

No One Injured After Gunman Opens Fire On Xbox Studio

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Xbox-owned studio inXile Entertainment confirmed that no developers were hurt

A gunman opened fire on the Tustin, California building where the Xbox-owned studio inXile Entertainment operates last week. inXile has confirmed that no one was hurt when the incident occurred.

The shots were fired at inXile Entertainment's building around 1:13 p.m. on Nov. 11, with the Tustin Police Department calling it a drive-by shooting on its official Facebook account. That same post reveals that "Extensive crime scene processing was conducted, and detectives immediately began investigating to identify and locate a suspect," and that a suspect was taken into custody on the morning of Nov. 13.

Aftermath then reported on the incident on Nov. 13, confirming the details with Tustin police, who said the investigation is still ongoing. While inXile Entertainment has yet to address the situation on social media, the studio did provide a statement to both Aftermath and Kotaku.

"We’re relieved that no one was hurt at our inXile office. Our focus is on supporting our team, and we are working closely with law enforcement as they investigate," the statement from inXile Entertainment reads.

According to an X post spotted by Kotaku, inXile Entertainment moved into the Tustin-based studio in 2020. This move came less than two years after the RPG developer, which was founded by Interplay's Brian Fargo in 2002, was acquired by Xbox Game Studios. You most likely know the studio for its work on the Wasteland games, The Bard's Tale 4: Barrows Deep, and Torment: Tides of Numenera.

The studio's last game, the post-apocalyptic role-playing game Wasteland 3, came out in 2020. Microsoft still owns inXile Entertainment, and it is currently working on Clockwork Revolution. As of now, this situation has not had an impact on the game's 2027 release window on PC and Xbox Series X. More importantly, everyone who is working on the game at that studio is safe.

Spoilers for Nathan Fielder’s You Can See Everything can be seen throughout this story. Seriously, though, it’s worth the three-hour runtime, and is maybe the best movie to come out so far this year.

One of the main points of contention in You Can See Everything, the latest A24 movie from Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, is the fact Elizabeth Holmes and her set-up-to-be-the-villain partner Billy Evans seemed to be going back to the drawing board and creating a system that sounded like a Theranos 2.0. Obviously, several years have passed since Holmes went to prison, and her partner is now working on a new business. He bristled at a comparison to Theranos.

When journalist Matt Gutman asked Evans about “Theranos 3” in an already-viral Sunday Mornings interview, the entrepreneur and husband wanted to set the record straight. Even prior to the interview finally airing in full, we know the man felt his You Can See Everything portrayal was “crazy.” And I’m not even talking about whatever happened with that shower scene.

Per Evans, Gutman is actually a bit behind the times. The journalist asked about the project we see in the documentary, a project that had Evans and Holmes attempting to fix the technology that led to Theranos dealing with legal troubles by approaching the problem in a different way. According to the scientist, that project is dead, and it’s dead for one big reason: The tech was too expensive.

No. I am not working with Elizabeth on [my current project]. Important distinction from the movie: That was a different idea. Completely different technology.

Gutman interrupted and asked, “So, we’re talking about Theranos 3 now?” Evans shook his head, and responded:

It’s not Theranos. At the time we were filming, we were talking about basically using microscopes, taking really good camera images of bacteria, and then using computer vision like driverless cars use to determine that’s a car, that’s a bus, that’s a person… So, that was kind of the idea. Turns out, that the cost was just too high.

Shortly after Holmes was in prison, they scrapped the idea we see them chatting about in You Can See Everything. Despite the way the now-infamous egg scene portrays Evans, he said here he spent about seven months worrying about the kids and life in Texas. Where the kids would go to preschool, what hotels to stay at while visiting Holmes in prison.

Then, he got back to it. Now, he is still working on "the problem," but he feels his research is not the same thing.

Once I finally settled and I regained like my strength and my energy back, I started thinking about, ‘Well, that last one didn’t work. But how could it? And my background’s in photonics, lasers. You know it’s a big thing in the movie, lasers. So I started reading research papers, and I’m now working with a group of scientists and other people to solve the same problem in a different way.

Despite Holmes preparing for life in prison, there was a shared sense between the two the work would go on, and Fielder said they both had optimism while filming she may not end up behind bars. Ultimately, from what Evans seems to be saying, the former happened, though the latter did not pan out as expected.

2 Hedge Fund Managers Want Jensen Huang Back. Could He Return as Nvidia CEO?

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The calls for Huang's return are getting louder. Will it matter?

Jensen Huang's departure has shocked the business world and left Nvidia (NVDA -2.01%) investors wondering what happens next.

Shares of the AI chip leader tumbled 15% last week as the drama unfolded around Huang, and are down a third from their peak in October.

However, over a longer time frame, Nvidia has been one of the greatest engines of wealth creation in human history. Prior to the recent slide, Nvidia had added roughly $5 trillion in market value over the last decade, and its investors don't want to see that continue to erode.

Now, two prominent hedge fund managers, Brad Gerstner of Altimeter Capital and Gavin Baker of Atreides Management, are calling for the board to consider bringing back Huang.

In a letter to the board, the two investors asked the directors "to pursue a slow, measured, and deliberate process regarding the future of Nvidia that leaves all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang." They added, "The Founder and longtime incredible leader of this extraordinary company has earned the right for a deliberate process that avoids any needless rush to replace him before all the facts are known."

Jensen Huang speaking on stage. Image source: Getty Images.

In addition to that letter, an open petition that calls for him to be reinstated has received more than 80,000 signatures, and hundreds of Nvidia employees have signed a letter calling for Huang to be brought back. Clearly, there's a strong interest in his return.

This call comes at the same time that the federal government said that it has not determined that Alice Huang, the Megaspeed executive with whom Jensen Huang had an affair, is a spy. In other words, she was not directly employed by Beijing and may instead have been an "asset," someone who could have passed along information to Chinese intelligence.

It could take months and multiple investigations to determine if Jensen Huang or Nvidia broke any laws or what, if anything, may have been leaked to Alice Huang.

At minimum, for Huang to return, there would need to be a full exoneration by the federal government over the chip export ordeal, and that will take time. Jensen has agreed to testify before Congress.

Nvidia announced Huang's resignation Thursday morning after meeting the night before to reportedly oust him, and it's not clear if Huang willingly agreed to walk away in the end, or if he was pushed out.

According to Bloomberg, Huang seemed to express remorse before he resigned, saying, "I'm a piece of sh--." The episode has been humiliating for him, and the resignation may have come to avoid more dirty laundry being aired in the media. After the resignation, Reuters reported a third affair with an unnamed employee, and there could be more.

In other words, it's unclear if Huang wants his job back right now.

At this point, Huang's return seems like a long shot and is not a reason to buy the stock. Bringing him back could lead to further chaos at the company, especially while Nvidia is being investigated by regulators.

The calls could grow louder, though, depending on how the company performs without him, and if enough shareholders want him back, they could install a new board to do so.

Rather than indulging in that drama, investors are better off focusing on the facts on the ground and what they mean for the future of Nvidia. We'll get more insights when the company reports third-quarter earnings on Tuesday during the earnings call. How CFO and interim CEO Colette Kress handles the call and what information she shares should help signal where the company goes from here. The only thing that's certain for Nvidia is there's plenty of uncertainty ahead.

How long will your NVIDIA GPU be supported?

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

NVIDIA can support GeForce GPUs for years, but Game Ready updates, security patches and OS support don't all end at once.

Graphics cards do not have an expiration date on the box. That would make it easier to upgrade, but much more depressing.

NVIDIA also does not guarantee a specific number of years of driver support for all GeForce GPUs. Rather, support is largely dependent on the architecture your card uses. At time of writing, Turing, Ampere, Ada and Blackwell GPUs are still on the normal Game Ready Driver track, which includes new features, game optimizations, performance improvements and bug fixes. That means that even though the RTX 2060 was released in 2019, it continues to receive regular driver updates.

Older cards have started moving down the ladder. In October 2025, Maxwell, Pascal and Volta GPUs stopped receiving Game Ready updates, but NVIDIA will continue to offer critical security updates until October 2028. Pascal and Volta had nine years of Game Ready support, whereas Maxwell lasted for 11 years.

That's a pretty respectable run, but what comes next and how could it impact your system?

NVIDIA does have a neatly defined three-phase software support lifecycle. Unfortunately, it's about NVIDIA vGPU software; not about the GeForce card in your gaming PC.

NVIDIA vGPU is a virtualization solution, mostly for enterprise use (data centers and professional deployments), that requires an active software or support subscription to receive software support. Phase I, also known as Full Support, includes new features, performance enhancements, security patches and bug fixes. Phase II is the next three years of Extended Full Support, with the same coverage, and Phase III is the final three years of Maintenance Support, where the GPU remains on its last supported software branch and receives maintenance, security fixes and bug fixes as NVIDIA sees fit.

GeForce support is far less ceremonial. NVIDIA has moved older architectures off its primary Game Ready branch, but doesn't publish a single lifespan for all generations.

That can also make model names confusing. The GTX 750 Ti is a Maxwell card and will continue to receive security updates until October 2028, whereas the more powerful GTX 780 Ti is based on the older Kepler architecture, whose published security-update window ended in September 2024. A good rule of thumb: bigger number equals older architecture. GPU naming is weird that way.

The end of Game Ready support sounds a lot more dramatic than it normally is.

Your graphics card does not suddenly refuse to boot up Windows or stop running all of the games you already have installed. Existing drivers continue to work. What goes away is the constant flow of optimizations for new games, feature additions, performance tweaks and regular bug fixes, which can become more noticeable as newer software expects newer drivers.

Security-only support is still meaningful though. NVIDIA has already issued dedicated Maxwell, Pascal and Volta security drivers during 2026, so these cards haven't simply been abandoned.

Another clock is ticking too: your operating system. The last full Game Ready and Studio driver for Windows 10 will be available in October 2026, according to NVIDIA. Windows 10 systems will get quarterly security updates and critical bug fixes until October 2029. So even a newer RTX GPU might lose the normal Game Ready update stream due to Windows and not the hardware.

For an RTX 2060 owner, driver support alone is not much of a reason to start looking for a new GPU. Performance and VRAM limits or a lack of access to newer features may become bigger issues first. NVIDIA hasn't confirmed a new GeForce generation for 2027 either, despite reports previously pointing to late-2027 mass production for its next architecture.

→ Baca artikel asli

Robots now run AI-written code without an AI calling the shots, succeeding 92% of the time

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Robots completed 221 of 240 trials using Claude-written programs for tasks such as uncapping bottles. Human help was available during development, not execution.

Robot programs developed by Anthropic’s Claude Opus 5.5 coding agent completed 221 of 240 physical evaluation trials, ACE-CaP-0 researchers reported in a study posted November 12. The result shows that task-specific skills can persist in code and run without the coding agent choosing each action.

The researchers behind ACE-CaP-0, a method for developing robot control programs, reported a 92.1% success rate across eight tasks. Each program was frozen before 30 separate evaluation trials, with no further updates. Human guidance and demonstrations were available during development.

The experiment separates program development from repeated execution. The agent's work produced a program that could be used repeatedly. During evaluation, that program still used perception and control systems to respond to observations, while its decision rules stayed fixed. Neither a human nor the coding agent remained in the execution loop.

ACE-CaP-0 separates assisted program development from repeated execution with live perception and control.

Human assistance is available during development; the frozen task program runs without a human or coding agent in the execution loop.

Authors' preprint account. Development permits human assistance; demonstration-collection resets are excluded from the ten-reset budget. Repeated execution on tested tasks does not establish reuse under new conditions.

Source-bound qualitative process diagram from sections 2 to 4. Arrows show code revision and runtime sensing relationships, not timing, trial quantities or comparative efficiency.

Authors' preprint account. Development permits human assistance; demonstration-collection resets are excluded from the ten-reset budget. Repeated execution on tested tasks does not establish reuse under new conditions.

Author-reported successful trials out of 30 for each frozen program. These were physical evaluation trials, separate from development; Table 1, November 12 preprint.

The physical setup used two Agilex PIPERX arms and three cameras. Tasks included inserting a charger, uncapping a bottle and packing shoes. Charger insertion succeeded in all 30 trials; bread packing, the weakest result, succeeded in 25. Mean physical execution times across tasks ranged from 0.97 to 2.27 minutes.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

During development, the coding agent wrote, ran, inspected and revised its code using physical feedback. It could ask for textual advice or demonstrations in which a person physically guided the robot arms. Those demonstrations supplied video, arm trajectories and key joint poses.

Development allowed at most ten human-assisted scene resets per task. Resets used to collect demonstrations were excluded, and the budget counted scene resets rather than all robot executions or demonstrations.

The agent could also use simulated experiments when they helped resolve a development question. In a charger-insertion comparison, on-demand simulation produced a program with 100% evaluation success, versus 86.7% for development without simulation. The comparison used one development run per condition.

That comparison also illustrates a cost trade-off. Development took 2.6 hours with on-demand simulation versus 3.7 hours without it, while output-token usage increased from 730,000 to 1,090,000. The time and token figures leave total development costs unresolved; the researchers call for accounting that includes demonstration effort.

Repeating evaluation trials measures how the resulting program performs. Establishing how consistently the development process produces an equally capable program requires separate development runs across tasks.

ACE-CaP-0 leaves behind task-specific programs that can execute repeatedly with fresh observations and fixed decision rules. The study provides evidence for that form of skill reuse after human-assisted development. Testing those routines under new conditions is the next step toward establishing how broadly they can be reused.

Wu-Tang Clan, Billy Idol, Phil Collins Enter Rock Hall at All-Star Induction Ceremony

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It was difficult to imagine who could possibly top Wu-Tang Clan’s performance at the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame induction on Saturday night at Los Angeles’ Peacock Theater. Following a spirited introduction from Chris Rock, all living members of the Staten Island hip-hop group hit the stage to perform a medley of some of their biggest hits, including “C.R.E.A.M.” and “Wu-Tang Clan Ain’t Nuthing ta F’ Wit.” The audience was on its collective feet screaming “Wu-Tang! Wu-Tang!” repeatedly as the mighty crew wielded their lyrical swords.

But then came the Red Hot Chili Peppers, who were part of Rick Rubin’s induction. Cosplaying Rubin (who, after an incredibly theatrical induction speech by Neil Young, accepted his honor in shorts, sandals and rose-tinted sunglasses), Anthony Kiedis, John Frusciante, Flea and Chad Smith erupted into their Blood Sugar Sex Magik single “Give It Away” donning fake white beards. After a short technical issue with Flea’s instrument, the ever-limber bassist and Kiedis continued to somehow defy aging and rocked it like it was 1991.

The high octane performances kept coming at a dizzying pace. The speeches were long (sans Rubin) and the event as usual ran well over four hours. But as Wu-Tang Clan and Rubin joined fellow honorees Queen Latifah, New Order/Joy Division, MC Lyte, Billy Idol, Iron Maiden, Luther Vandross, Sade, Oasis, Phil Collins, Celia Cruz, Fela Kuti, Gram Parsons, Linda Creed, Arif Mardin and Jimmy Miller, the excitement of “what’s next” made time disappear.

Of course several inductees opted not to show up (Oasis, New Order’s Bernard Sumner, Gillian Gilbert and Stephen Morris, Sade and Iron Maiden) and some of them were being inducted posthumously, but the Rock Hall was still able to make the evening magical with surprise presenters and performances.

U2 opened the show with “Silencio,” the lead single from their latest album, Carnaval de Luz, and their 1984 hit “Pride (In the Name of Love).” Jack Black joined St. Vincent, Metallica’s Kirk Hammett and Robert Trujillo and Rage Against the Machine’s Brad Wilk for an Iron Maiden tribute. MC Lyte came out firing on all cylinders for “Boom! Shake the Room” before she was joined by Queen Latifah for “U.N.I.T.Y.” and “Ladies First” featuring Megan Thee Stallion and Remy Ma. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

Usher sailed through Luther Vandross’ hits “So Amazing,” “Here and Now” and “Never Too Much” and blossoming British chanteuse Sienna Spiro had the nearly impossible task of covering Sade’s “Smooth Operator.” With her raspy vocals and slow, sensuous execution, it worked, though there was a sense nobody would have wanted to trade places with her.

Blink-182’s Travis Barker inducted the ailing Phil Collins. He talked about the immense impact Collins had on him as an aspiring drummer, saying, “He wasn’t just an incredible drummer. He was also a singer-songwriter and one of the biggest solo artists in the world. Songs like ‘Sussudio,’ ‘Take Me Home,’ ‘Easy Lover’ and ‘Against All Odds’ was the soundtrack to my childhood. And then there’s ‘In The Air Tonight,’ his first solo single with arguably the most badass drum fill of all time. You don’t even have to be a drummer to know that fill. When that part comes on, everybody plays air drums.”

With a cane and the aid of an assistant, Collins made his way to the stage and was greeted by a standing ovation. The 75-year-old appeared more feeble than anticipated, making the moment even more emotional. “I’m really not as bad as it looks,” he quipped. “I’m much better.” Related Content Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony to Kick Off as Planned Amid FAA Grounding Flights Debacle Iron Maiden’s Nicko McBrain Recalls Spinal Tap Moments Touring for ‘Somewhere in Time’ John Mulaney Has Many Thoughts on the 2026 Rock Hall Inductees — And Picks Next Year’s Class 2026 Rock & Roll Hall of Fame: 10 Burning Questions About Oasis, Sade, Phil Collins, and More

Collins then gave a tearful speech about his life and career, saying, “I grew up just wanting to play the drums. Everything else has been a really happy accident. I can’t believe, when I look back, how life has gone.” He also addressed the whisperings of an upcoming tour. While he didn’t confirm it would (or even could happen), he did end with, “You haven’t heard the last of me yet. I can promise you that.” Collins was unable to perform, but Cynthia Erivo did her best rendition of “In the Air Tonight” with Barker behind the kit.

Oprah Winfrey was on deck to induct Vandross. She spoke about her personal connection to the singer, saying, “Luther always gave me great counsel and taught me a lot about what it means to live well. Used to tell me, ‘Girl, use all your good dishes for yourself.’ One of my favorite songs that I heard for the first time as I was leaving my studio in Chicago, really late one night, someone on my team had handed me a new cassette tape, and I put it in for the drive home.”

“Yes, it was the eighties when cars had cassette tapes, and it was a brand-new Luther Vandross tape. And when his smash hit song ‘Power of Love’ came on, I was already almost at my front door. But I kept driving around the block, driving around the block, around the block to hear it over and over again. I love that song, still do.”

She compared listening to “Power of Love” as a “spiritual experience,” adding, “Luther Vandross was somebody who sang with such passion and soul; it could reach something deep inside that you didn’t even know was waiting to reach.”

It felt like déjà vu when Trent Reznor stepped up to the mic to induct Joy Division and New Order. Seven years ago, the Nine Inch Nails maestro welcomed the Cure into the esteemed Hall and admitted he’d been “ambivalent” about “certain award ceremonies.”

But there he was in his thick, black-rimmed glasses recounting the history of both bands. Moments later, Peter Hook stood alone at the podium without Sumner, Gilbert or Morris, making it painfully clear the band was unable to put their differences aside.

“Now I’m here and I realize how special it is,” Hook said. “It’s like being part of a family, which I’m delighted about. Now I’m on my own, but I’m not alone … it’s been a wild 50 years, and I’ve been happy, I’ve been mad and I’ve been heartbroken in equal measures. But I’m still fucking here.” Hook then picked up his bass and joined Deftones and Chvrches’ Lauren Mayberry for “Love Will Tear Us Apart” and “Bizarre Love Triangle” with the same fervor he’s harnessed for decades.

The Oasis segment, however, was a sore spot. Liam and Noel Gallagher already declined the invitation, so their absence was expected. But Stephen Wilson Jr., a country artist with no ties to British rock whatsoever, performing “Wonderwall” almost felt like a slight to Oasis. Wilson knew it, too. Backstage in the press room, he was reminded that the Gallagher brothers will likely see the performance and there was a chance they’d roast him for it. He said he had “thick skin” and would be “honored” if he were to become the target of a Gallagher brothers’ joke, but coupled with the awkward introduction by Better Call Saul actor Bob Odenkirk (who satirized the Gallagher brothers’ “cheeky” attitudes for the HBO comedy series Mr. Show in the 1990s), it seemed like Oasis’ absence was just too massive of a hole to fill.

That’s not to say the Rock Hall didn’t try: Billy Idol was ushered into the Hall by No Doubt’s Gwen Stefani and Tony Kanal. Stefani called Idol’s music a “time machine” and recalled listening to him in the early 1980s. Idol’s speech didn’t fail to entertain either as he sometimes struggled to read the teleprompter.

“Rock & roll says we can be heroes,” Idol said. “In my case I called myself Billy Idol so I could be my own hero.” He and longtime guitarist Steve Stevens proceeded to perform “Rebel Yell” and “Eyes Without a Face” as a duet with Stefani.

In the press room, Idol didn’t field any questions but posed for the cameras with his signature lip snarl and fist pumps, though Stevens spoke at length.

“Music is such a universal thing that I got the opportunity at some point to stay at Billy’s parents’ house and got to see his record collection, his adolescent William Broad record collection, and they were a lot of the same records that I grew up on,” Stevens said. “We’re roughly the same age, so it was David Bowie, T. Rex, Slade, Sweet Reed. We grew up [differently], but the music and things that inspired us were very much the same.”

Stevens also talked about his hopes for future generations of rock stars, saying, “There’s not that many true rock stars around to replace a Billy Idol. Hopefully, we’ll inspire people, and there’ll be another Steve Stevens … I’ve seen them on YouTube playing the solo for ‘Rebel Yell’ and sometimes playing it better than I can, so hopefully that’s the next generation. From what I understand, a lot of the record companies are looking at, you know, ‘Wow, we really have to develop artists again,’ because just having the numbers of viewers on TikTok or something is not a career.”

The crescendo of the evening came as Public Enemy’s Chuck D, with his inimitable and commanding voice, introduced Aerosmith to the stage. Steven Tyler, Joe Perry and the rest of the band started playing the first few notes to “Walk This Way” before Tyler suddenly stopped and told Perry there was “something missing.” Just as they’d done at The Hollywood Bowl two weeks ago, Run and DMC appeared from opposite sides of the stage to perform the crossover smash, elating the crowd.

While the 2026 iteration of the annual ceremony was mostly a success, a few things were glossed over. Gram Parsons and Ed Sullivan’s segments felt incomplete. Rumors Paul McCartney and Ringo Starr were somehow involved with the Sullivan induction ran rampant backstage, considering the Beatles’ rich history with The Ed Sullivan Show, it would have made sense. But alas, it came and went without much fanfare. Trending Stories 'SNL' Cold Open: Jensen Huang, Tech CEOs Get a Crash Course in Female Spies 'SNL' Weekend Update Roasts Trump Admin Over Grounded Flights, Jensen Huang Affairs Could 'Rubber Soul' Be the Best Beatles Album? (Yes) Lana Del Rey, Dua Lipa, Zara Larsson Contribute New Music to 'GTA VI: The Album'

The In Memoriam portion paid tribute to a long list of musical figures we’ve lost, including Afrika Bambaataa, Rob Base, producer Bob Power, Traffic’s Dave Mason, ZZ Top’s Frank Beard and the Grateful Dead’s Bob Weir, who deserved much more than a photo flashing across a screen. Dolly Parton, however, was allotted a significant highlight reel, capturing the magic that made Dolly Dolly.

But the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame exists to ensure they are remembered long after they are gone. It’s not perfect and maybe they don’t always get it “right.” Gene Simmons may still be infuriated that hip-hop and R&B are welcome. Iron Maiden still might scoff at the idea as a whole. The Gallagher brothers might stick up their middle fingers. But still, it goes on — what’s more rock and roll than that?

Waldorf Astoria Chooses Upscale Denver Neighborhood for Its First Colorado Residences

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Legendary hospitality brand Waldorf Astoria has chosen a neighborhood ten minutes from downtown Denver for its first foray into residential living in Colorado.

Located at 185 Steele Street, with 37 private residences ranging from studios to penthouses, Waldorf Astoria Residences Cherry Creek is slated to open in early 2028.

With architecture by SAR+, interiors by BAMO, and conceptual design by Carlos Ott, prices start at $1.5 million for a studio and cap out at $13 million for the largest penthouse.

While the luxury leader could have set down in a high-end ski haven like Aspen, Vail, or Telluride, the company instead went with Cherry Creek, a dense, walkable retail corridor five miles from the center of Denver.

The neighborhood's median price per square foot is $293, according to Realtor.com data, compared to $334 for the overall Denver metropolitan area.

Featuring two major commercial destinations—Cherry Creek North and Cherry Creek Shopping Center—the global brand felt this was the place to make its residential debut in the Rocky Mountain state.

The brand's housing portfolio is set to nearly triple, reaching 34 properties and 3,498 units by 2030, according to the company.

Residences are particularly clustered in Florida, with condo buildings in Pompano Beach, St. Petersburg, Sarasota, and Miami. Other locations include New York City; Atlanta, GA; Chicago, IL; Las Vegas, NV; Park City, UT; and Maui, HI.

Cherry Creek, in a small, dense 16-block radius, may seem an unexpected choice to join in this world renowned group of cities, but the developer says it as an easy decision.

"We recognized early on that Denver was ready for a world-class, branded luxury residential offering, and Cherry Creek was the natural fit," Ryan Shear, managing partner at developer Property Markets Group (PMG), told Realtor.com.

"The neighborhood’s established luxury market, vibrant retail and dining scene, and distinctive character aligned perfectly with the Waldorf Astoria brand and the kind of project we aimed to deliver."

"Cherry Creek has long been one of Denver's most prestigious neighborhoods," Dawn Raymond of Kentwood Real Estate, who is heading up sales in the complex, tells Realtor.com.

She touts the district's central location, walkability, eye-catching architecture, high-end shopping options, art galleries, and a restaurant scene that has "exploded" in the past five years.

According to Denver's tourism website, the neighborhood boasts 300 stores, 75 cafes and restaurants, 50 spas and salons, and five hotels.

"It's really the walkability that appeals to everyone," she says. "It's like a large village. To be able to live in the neighborhood, and walk out the door in the morning, grab a cup of coffee or go work out before going into the office, it's very much a live-work place."

Raymond, who has long represented top tier properties in the area, says that there are only three other luxury condos in the neighborhood—and none of them offer the level of service and amenities that the Waldorf does.

Affluent state residents were ready to pounce: An impressive 75% of the complex's units, or all but seven, are already sold. This includes four of the seven penthouses, as well as the most expensive penthouse, priced at $13 million.

Monthly HOA dues will be estimated about $1.50 per square foot, which will cover most amenities and services.

Raymond says that the developer, PMG, "leaned on" the local brokerages, asking them what well-heeled buyers in the area wanted.

"We had a whole list of things people were looking for," she says. Top of the list for amenities was a fitness center—and the complex will have a wellness center with massage, sauna and steam rooms, as well as the now-requisite cold plunge.

"A lot of people were asking about that," she says with a laugh. "It's like taking two shots of espresso."

There's also a resort rooftop pool, signature restaurant (still being negotiated), owners' lounge, and the Waldorf's signature Peacock Alley-inspired lounge and bar.

These affluent buyers are also busy—and crave as frictionless a lifestyle as possible.

"They value time, convenience, personalized service," she says. "That's really a hallmark of the Waldorf Astoria name."

To that end, the concierge services will include valet, housekeeping, pet services, laundry, parcel reception, doorman and bellman, security, automotive detailing, chef services, and more.

Personal concierge services are "a la carte," and could include requests such as finding a hotel for visiting family members, or procuring tickets to a hot theatrical production. An initial concierge meeting with a buyer will help pinpoint services that might be needed, says the broker.

Ownership also comes with discounts at both Waldorf Astoria and parent company Hilton hotels worldwide.

"What surprised me is how many local people were buying," says the agent. "I was thinking we'd be getting people from all over the country—which we did—but the initial run was all local people."

She attributes the intense local interest to elite Colorado residents—who already own a ski chalet, mountain estate, or sprawling ranch—also wanting a "city place," as their second or even third abode.

But until a complex came along with a bold name like Waldorf Astoria, they couldn't find a place that met their high standards.

"A lot of the buyers had looked at the other condo buildings," she says, but found them lacking. "They were waiting for something truly spectacular to come along."

The Waldorf Astoria was "literally the perfect brand to come in," she says. "It's just brought so much excitement."

Beyond generally being locals, the condo's buyers are a "diverse group," from CEOs to techies to "oil and gas people" to ranchers. "It's quite a mix of people," she says.

Since most of the buyers will be using the pads as pieds-à-terre, they wanted to know that everything will be taken care of for them awhile they were away, says the broker.

"They trust the name and brand. It's very special."

The 8 Best Soft Sci-Fi Movies in Film History

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The label 'soft sci-fi' refers to sci-fi stories that shift the focus away from strict scientific plausibility and instead place a premium on people, culture, and society. They're not that interested in explaining the nitty-gritty of their advanced tech or alien creatures. The speculative elements exist to bring out the themes or reveal something about human nature.

With that in mind, this list looks at some of the best movies in this subgenre, from intimate romances grand to space operas, the wacky time-travel of Back to the Future to the existential cybernoir of Blade Runner. Bangers, all.

“Meet me in Montauk.” Although this one has a speculative premise involving fictional technology, it's really a story about relationships. After discovering that his former girlfriend Clementine (Kate Winslet) has undergone a procedure to remove her memories of their relationship, main character Joel (Jim Carrey) decides to do the same. Most of the film then unfolds inside his collapsing memories as the procedure progresses, which writer Charlie Kaufman and director Michel Gondry (both masters of the offbeat) cleverly present in non-linear order.

Joel revisits arguments, disappointments, tenderness, embarrassment, and ordinary moments that seemed insignificant when they happened. In the process, he begins to realize that losing the painful parts of the relationship also means losing everything meaningful about it. As a result, Eternal Sunshine of the Spotless Mind winds up being a madcap but surprisingly touching psychodrama.

“The heart's not like a box that gets filled up. It expands in size the more you love.” This is one of the most perceptive movies ever made about AI, precisely because it avoids the usual Skynet-like 'evil robot' storyline. Instead, we get something more wistful and intimate. Joaquin Phoenix leads the cast as Theodore Twombly, a lonely writer living in a near-future Los Angeles who installs an advanced operating system that names herself Samantha (voiced by Scarlett Johansson).

Samantha learns extraordinarily quickly, developing curiosity, humor, insecurity, desire, and an increasingly complex relationship with Theodore. Director Spike Jonze refuses to treat that romance as a simple joke about a man falling in love with his computer. Ultimately, Her paints a compellingly prescient picture of isolation in the digital age, as well as a cautionary tale about the inability of any machine to truly replicate human connection.

“Roads? Where we're going, we don't need roads.” Back to the Future devotes remarkably little effort to making time travel scientifically credible. Doc Brown (Christopher Lloyd) invents the flux capacitor after hitting his head on a bathroom sink, installs it inside a DeLorean, and discovers that traveling through time requires reaching 88 miles per hour. That's essentially all audiences need to know, because director Robert Zemeckis understands that explaining the physics would only slow down the endlessly entertaining, ingeniously constructed plot.

The solution requires our likable hero Marty McFly (Michael J. Fox) to play matchmaker for two teenagers who will eventually become his own mother and father, while simultaneously finding enough power to return home. The time travel itself makes no real sense and functions more like something out of a fairy tale than a typical sci-fi flick, but that's all part of the charm.

“Everything that comes true is an expression of your true wishes.” This cryptic gem from Andrei Tarkovsky bears some sci-fi trappings, but approaches them with a meditative sensibility. In Stalker, three men (Alexander Kaidanovsky, Anatoly Solonutsyn, and Nikolai Grinko) are sent into an eerily abandoned landscape known as the Zone, where the normal rules of reality may no longer apply. The Stalker makes his living illegally guiding people into this forbidden territory.

His latest clients are known simply as the Writer and the Professor, both of whom want to reach a mysterious Room supposedly capable of granting a person's deepest desire. Their journey gradually becomes less about reaching a physical destination than confronting what each man actually believes. In other words, this is philosophical sci-fi where clear answers are in desperately short supply. It's poetic and elliptical, leaving the viewer to draw their own conclusions.

“Dreams make good stories, but everything important happens when we're awake.” The original Dune novels are landmarks of soft sci-fi, almost science fantasy, really, and Denis Villeneuve did a great job of carrying that over to his film adaptations, while still making them feel modern. He weaves a colorful, thematically rich tale of prophecy and personality cults, focused on Paul (Timothée Chalamet), the scion of House Atreides, ordered to take control of Arrakis.

Although the story takes place in a far-flung future, the vibe is mythological rather than coldly high-tech. (Indeed, we don't see any digital devices at all). We get grnithopters, shields, spice harvesting machines, and interstellar travel, but the movie doesn't get bogged down in explaining how it all works. Instead, the emphasis is on imperialism, political manipulation, ecological exploitation, religion, and Paul's growing awareness that the visions surrounding him may have horrifying consequences.

“Unfortunately, no one can be told what the Matrix is. You have to see it for yourself.” The Matrix contains enough philosophy, martial arts, cyberpunk imagery, religious symbolism, and leather coats to fuel decades of interpretation. Keanu Reeves turns in perhaps his most iconic performance as Neo, a programmer who learns that humanity has been imprisoned by machines inside a simulated reality, all so they can be exploited for the machines' ends.

It's a killer premise that draws on real problems from philosophy, which the Wachowskis gleefully fuse with ideas borrowed from anime, Hong Kong action cinema, cyberpunk literature, and mythology. So much of the movie has become world-famous (like the red and blue pills), and its vision of a future where people live entirely in an online, digital world manipulated by bots now feels uncomfortably close to home.

“Do. Or do not. There is no try.” Few sequels build so well on their predecessor's foundation. A New Hope gave us a galaxy of Jedi, droids, smugglers, and impossible spacecraft, and The Empire Strikes Back gave those ideas a darker, deeper treatment. Here, the heroes lose repeatedly, and those failures make their eventual journey more meaningful. After the Rebel Alliance is driven from its base on Hoth, the central characters are separated.

Han (Harrison Ford) and Leia (Carrie Fisher) flee the Empire aboard the Millennium Falcon while Luke (Mark Hamill) travels to Dagobah to train under Yoda. Again, the aesthetic verges on science fantasy. Faster-than-light travel, giant walking tanks, artificial intelligence, and inhabited asteroid fields exist alongside the mystical Force. What matters is how those elements serve the characters. Then comes one of cinema's most famous revelations, completely altering Luke's understanding of both his enemy and himself.

“It's too bad she won't live! But then again, who does?” The plot of Blade Runner has noir bones in a futuristic skin: former police officer Rick Deckard (Harrison Ford) is tasked with hunting replicants, bioengineered beings created for dangerous labor and prohibited from living on Earth. This mission unfolds in a future overflowing with extraordinary technology, yet Ridley Scott is far less interested in how any of it works than in atmosphere and theme.

The key question is: what does humanity mean once technology can reproduce something almost indistinguishable from a person? That idea turns a noir detective story into one of the most haunting sci-fi movies ever made. A big part of that is thanks to Rutger Hauer's brilliant performance as replicant leader Roy Batty. Roy is frightening, charismatic, and ultimately deeply tragic, making this a story of ideas rather than gadgets.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Haven't seen Stalker yet, on the surface it looks so drab and depressing. But these all qualify as great.

Horu is expected to launch at the end of next year.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I could claim that I love real-time strategy games for their fast-paced decision-making. But what I truly love is building massive armies and wreaking havoc on an apocalyptic scale. So when I saw the trailer for Horu (via RPS), an in-development RTS that promises "hundreds of thousands of units" on screen, it went on the wishlist faster than you can say 'Zerg Rush'.

Horu is an upcoming strategy game inspired by Total War and Star Wars: Empire at War, aiming to blend the colossal battles of the former with the galaxy-scale strategy of the latter. It challenges players to establish their own interstellar empire, building vast space fleets and huge land armies to assume ultimate dominion of the cosmos.

While it's the spectacle of Horu that caught my attention, with clouds of spaceships trading laser fire and oceans of little men getting obliterated by artillery, there are a couple of more specific ideas and mechanics that also sound deeply exciting. Unlike the games that inspired it, Horu aspires to be fully real-time, with battles playing out simultaneously across the solar system. This includes both land and space battles, letting you do things like punch your dropships through an enemy's orbital fleet to land an army on the surface while the battle in space rages on.

This sounds rad, but also like it might be challenging to keep track of. Thankfully, Horu promises a few mechanics to keep the scale manageable. This includes a "drop zone system" that lets you assign a drop zone on a planet and queue up a set of orders, which the assigned army will then carry out automatically.

Other planned features include world colonisation and development, the ability to build spy networks and conduct espionage missions, space magic that'll let you cast devastating spells on the battlefield, and a fully simulated economy and research tree. There's even a system that lets you create boarding parties to take control of enemy ships and add them to your fleet.

All of this built by a solo dev who goes by GlassBeaver, who build his own custom engine to develop the game. Development has been underway for several years, but GlassBeaver has now taken the game to Kickstarter "to help fun the remaining assets, sound and music". The campaign is already halfway to reaching its goal of £16,685, so it looks likely to get over the finish line.

There's no specific release date for Horu yet, but GlassBeaver plans for the game to launch at the end of 2027. The next twelve months are shaping up to be big for space strategy more broadly, with Dawn of War 4 due to launch in December, and Total War: Warhammer 40,000 expected sometime next year.
